## DEATH-STEP TABLE

| step where ideas die | count | example passes | general reason |
|---|---:|---|---|
| False-world test: positive moment Gram remains positive in a bad Beurling system | 1 | 1A | Gram positivity uses only nonnegative weights, not the zero geometry |
| Exact Euler-cycle identification: multiplicative lattice graph has composite square cycles | 1 | 1B | Commuting distinct prime steps create primitive graph cycles not indexed by a single prime |
| Functional-equation test: imposed reflection of probability laws is not the completed zeta functional equation | 1 | 1C | Symmetry of a symmetrized measure does not identify its transform with the completed L-function |
| Prime-exponent cubical Euler supertrace collapses to a constant | 2 | 2A, 3C | Matching each vertex and edge at the same exponent makes each local alternating factor 1 |
| Functional-equation test: finite Euler factors are not reflection invariant | 1 | 3B | A finite Euler product has no dual completion; for P=2, Z(2)=4/3 while Z(-1)=-1 |
| False-world test: formal Euler-product derivative also holds for bad Beurling systems | 1 | 3A | The local factor identity uses only a multiplicative prime system and therefore does not distinguish its zero locations |
| False-world test: Lorentzian signature survives bad Beurling zeros | 1 | 5A | The one-positive Hessian direction depends on coefficient positivity, not on the associated zero geometry |
| Lorentzian binomial local weights do not equal geometric Euler coefficients | 1 | 5A | Their logarithmic derivative has q/(1+q), while the Euler factor requires q/(1-q) |
| Geometric prime-power truncation violates the Lorentzian Hessian signature | 1 | 5C | For K=2 the local Hessian is positive definite, with two positive eigenvalues rather than at most one |
| Nonnegative prime polynomials erase independent Euler phases | 1 | 5A | Random local phases leave the nonnegative-coefficient Lorentzian cone unless they are discarded or supplied as extra data |
| Constant-strength prime-log delta scattering has the wrong local Euler amplitude | 1 | 4A | The reflection magnitude is g/sqrt(g^2+4k^2), varying with k, unlike the constant p^(-1/2) modulus on Re(s)=1/2 |
| Positive prime-log Schrödinger spectrum is independent of the model's zero geometry | 1 | 4A | Replacing primes by generalized primes preserves self-adjoint positivity even when the Beurling zeta has off-line zeros |
| Prime-length quantum graph repeats the assigned-amplitude construction | 1 | 4B | Prime lengths alone do not determine vertex scattering, gamma phase, or the target determinant |
| Xi-derived Hermite-Biehler positivity imports the target inequality | 1 | 4C | Positivity of the de Branges kernel requires the global Hermite-Biehler inequality, which encodes the desired zero geometry |
| Fermionic prime-step nilpotence deletes repeated prime powers | 1 | 2B | Exterior creation satisfies c_p^2=0, so it cannot encode p^k for k>=2 |
| Mobius boundary-square repeats divisor-lift scalar cost | 1 | 2C | Recovering Lambda in a linear component leaves the known oversized positive lift norm |
| Least-violation record compression is model-agnostic | 1 | 6A | The proof uses only minimality and a monotone envelope, so it persists for ordered false-world sequences without constraining their zeros |
| Superabundant exponent ordering controls an abundance maximum, not spectral data | 2 | 6B, 18A | Local prime-exponent swaps characterize record structure and force a no-hole support condition for a least violating integer, but do not provide an independent sign estimate or zero-selective constraint |
| Normalized Robin slack is the target inequality in ratio form | 1 | 6C | Defining the defect by dividing the exact divisor sum by the target bound relocates the desired sign without proving it |
| One fixed off-line zero contributes only a lower-order term to a broad positive prime interval | 1 | 7A | For fixed 1/2<β<1, its interval contribution is at most h X^(β−1)=o(h), so that single residue cannot overturn the pole-scale main term |
| Finite differences that annihilate the pole lose coefficientwise positivity | 1 | 7B | Any nontrivial finite-difference stencil has both signs; monotonicity of the original prime count gives no sign for the filtered statistic |
| A nonnegative Mellin packet cannot cancel the pole coefficient | 1 | 7C | For nonzero w≥0, its Mellin transform at the real pole is strictly positive; signed cancellation abandons the positive-count certificate |
| LCM jump invariant is exactly the Chebyshev prime-power sum | 1 | 8A | log lcm(1,…,N)=ψ(N), so the lattice statistic has no independent evaluation beyond the prime-power sum itself |
| SPF-rough Liouville bias mixes primes with rough semiprimes | 1 | 8B | At the N^(1/3) cutoff, λ is −1 on primes and +1 on semiprimes; the observed signed bias has no independent sign or asymptotic estimate |
| Local divisor divisibility norm is too small to meet the Robin ceiling | 1 | 18B | The exact divisor D(n)=∏_{p|n}(p−1) divides σ(n), but D(n)≤n<e^γ n log log n for n>5040; it supplies a lower bound far below the target upper threshold |
| Liouville divisor sum detects squares but carries no Robin magnitude information | 1 | 18C | ∑_{d|n}λ(d)=1_{n square}; this parity label contains no estimate for σ(n)/n and is unchanged as a formal exponent-parity identity in generalized-prime models |
| Gamma-completed Fock trace has no intrinsic duality or positive Xi spectrum | 1 | 19B | Multiplying the Euler trace by the known gamma/pole factor gives the completed scalar only after analytic continuation; the positive Fock Hamiltonian remains a real-energy system and does not make Xi zeros its eigenvalues |
| Polarizable Euler cohomology axioms put the desired conclusion into the package | 1 | 19C | Exact Xi determinant plus a positive polarization/unitary normalized Frobenius forces the target zero bound; asserting these together assumes the key arithmetic realization rather than constructing it |
| Square-root roughness prime selector re-expresses the prime count | 1 | 8C | P⁻(n)>√N is equivalent to n prime in n≤N, so the aggregate is π(N)−π(√N) itself |
| Symmetric prime-log Herglotz transform is zero-blind | 1 | 9A | Positive atoms imply the Pick property and reflection oddness in every generalized-prime system; the transform's poles are log-support points, not completed-zeta zeros |
| Pick positivity imposed on the logarithmic derivative imports zero-freeness | 1 | 9B | Requiring holomorphy/Pick behavior on Re(s)>1/2 assumes away poles caused by zeros in that half-plane rather than deriving their absence |
| Finite reflected Euler product repeats imposed symmetry | 1 | 9C | Z_P(s)Z_P(1−s) is symmetric by construction but its finite factors do not identify the completed Xi function or its zero set |
| Positive measure/thermal-trace certificates cannot discriminate false generalized-prime systems | 11 | 1A, 2A, 3A, 4A, 5A, 6A, 7A, 8B, 9A, 15A, 19A | Positivity derived only from nonnegative Euler weights or a positive thermal trace survives generalized-prime substitution, including systems with bad zeros; an additional zero-sensitive identity is required |
| Prime-translation Hilbert–Schmidt degree loses the Frobenius trace cross term | 1 | 11A | The regular trace vanishes on every nonidentity shift, so Tr((nA−mI)*(nA−mI)) has no mixed trace coefficient; its positivity is generic rather than prime-selective |
| Unit-mass prime-only moment state fails the first mixed-prime PSD test | 1 | 11A | With prime moments −log2/√2 and −log3/√3 and mixed moments zero, the {0,e2,e3,e2+e3} Gram matrix has eigenvalue 1−log2/√2−log3/√3<0 |
| First-atom local remainder check is finite-test data, not a selective positivity mechanism | 1 | 12A | A positive value for one compactly supported test only checks one scalar; matching finitely many prime atoms does not constrain the global zero geometry |
| A support-scaled prime cutoff has operator error exponentially larger than the recorded doubly-exponential positivity margin | 1 | 13A | For cutoff \(Y=e^{2a}/4\), a two-lobe test isolates a dyadic prime power and gives form-error norm at least \((\log2)e^{-a}\) |
| Dense rational-test enumeration gives only a one-sided negative-witness semidecision | 1 | 14A | Strict negativity is open under the fixed-support test topology, so a negative test has a rational-bump approximation detectable by interval arithmetic; the procedure need not terminate when the form is nonnegative |
| Finite nonnegative Weil Gram blocks do not certify positivity on the completed test space | 1 | 14C | Every computed principal block can be PSD while a negative direction first appears outside that finite span; completeness requires an independently proved uniform tail estimate |
| Robin inequality as a finite certificate reformulates the global target rather than independently proving it | 1 | 14B | A checked integer range is finite data, while asserting the Robin bound for all integers is equivalent to RH and cannot be used as an independent certificate |
| Brownian log-place feature map matches prime diagonals but not the linear Weil form as a positive norm | 1 | 15A | Its exact cross term is the Weil prime sum, but the square identity subtracts both the prime-channel Gram energy and the anchor energy; no complete-form estimate controls them |
| Brownian log-prime Green Gram loses unit contractivity at the second prime | 1 | 15A | For the declared kernel, lambda_max is 1.010915... already on {2,3} and 1.559715... on {2,3,5}; positivity of the Gram does not imply contraction |
| Positive scale averages do not transfer positivity from averaged forms to each scale | 1 | 13B | A nonnegative average can hide negative scales and its local positivity persists in false arithmetic models |
| Two-scale subtraction cannot universally cancel the full pole term while retaining a positive form | 1 | 13C | The dilation changes the pole multiplier \(2\cosh(rx/2)\) non-proportionally; exact cancellation is only trivial or sign-indefinite |

| Boundary-spike anatomy at the first new prime atom is gamma-dominated | 1 | 16A | For two normalized smooth packets at the endpoints of support radius log(5)/2+epsilon, only the p=5 atom survives and contributes -log(5)/sqrt(5), while the exact archimedean translation energy is at least exp(-0.05)log(0.1/(4epsilon)); this family has Q tending to +infinity, not a negative witness |
| Minimal-support onset does not imply a zero eigenvalue at the threshold | 1 | 16B | The support-restricted infimum need not be attained or continuous in the support radius; the known nonvanishing atom-entry operator norm prevents the asserted small-perturbation crossing argument |
| Endpoint-focused rational search remains a one-sided semidecision | 1 | 16C | Any strict negative test is detectable by density, but an endless sequence of nonnegative endpoint tests supplies no certificate for universal positivity; this is the Pass 14A limitation |
| Robin-slack prime-append increments do not have a fixed sign | 1 | 17A | The exact local increment is negative for some superabundant records and positive for another (e.g. n=277200, q=13); it therefore supplies no monotonicity contradiction |
| Prime-exponent square circulation is an exact gradient identity | 1 | 17B | The two orders of adjoining distinct primes telescope to the same endpoint slack, so the discrete square has zero circulation identically rather than a forced parity or sign defect |
| Positive-count pole cancellation again requires signed differencing | 1 | 17C | Any nontrivial finite difference that kills the smooth barrier has mixed-sign coefficients, so nonnegative divisor or prime counts no longer constrain its sign |

| Divisor-shell complement duality has no cutoff-compatible top degree | 1 | 21A | The involution d↦N/d depends on the selected top integer N; for N=6 it sends 1↦6, while for N=12 it sends 1↦12, so the finite pairings do not restrict compatibly under exhaustion |
| Group-completed prime inversion destroys the positive Gibbs trace | 1 | 21B | Inversion makes log-norm energy unbounded in both directions; for every β>0, e^(−βH) is unbounded on inverse powers and hence is not a positive trace-class heat operator |
| Divisor-interval cohomology recovers Möbius signs but not zero-sensitive positivity | 1 | 21C | The reduced Euler characteristic is μ(n), so its Dirichlet series is 1/ζ(s); the alternating supertrace is signed and the same poset identity holds for generalized-prime monoids |
| Capped geometric occupation polynomials manufacture central-line roots in every Euler system | 1 | 22A | The finite geometric sum has zeros only when p^(−z) is a nontrivial root of unity, forcing Re(z)=0; the same factorization holds for bad-zero Beurling systems, and the centered trace diverges as the cap grows |
| Norm-ball complement is not integer-valued or exhaustion-compatible | 1 | 22B | n↦X/n leaves the integer state set unless n|X; restricting to divisors returns the arbitrary top-element involution of 21A |
| Exponent-box Hodge supertrace collapses to Euler characteristic | 1 | 22C | The finite box is contractible and its ordinary alternating heat trace is 1; weighted alternatives require added data and persist for generalized-prime labels |
| Scalar normalization cannot tame the joint centered-box ratio | 1 | 23A | For fixed z1>z2>1, the exact ratio contains exp((z1-z2)K theta(P)/2) while the uncentered Euler-product ratio tends to a finite positive value; scalar factors cancel |
| Infinite capped prime boxes have divergent complement degree | 1 | 23B | Any infinitely many active coordinates with integer caps Kp>=1 give sum Kp log p>=sum log p=infinity; finite support is not an exhaustion |
| Uncentering removes the box reflection | 1 | 23C | The complement symmetry is exactly the exponential centering identity; the uncentered Euler trace is not even in z |
| Capped Gibbs variance is positive in false generalized-prime systems | 1 | 24B | The second log derivative is a variance for every positive local norm q>1, so its positivity survives systems with bad zeros |
| Centered occupation trace places the Euler exponent at the wrong symmetry center | 1 | 24B | Centering at z=0 gives an Euler limit ζ(z); writing z=s−1/2 makes it ζ(s−1/2), while using z=s gives reflection s↦−s rather than s↦1−s |
| Reflected diagonal prime transfers have disjoint trace-class half-planes | 1 | 25B | L_s requires Re(s)>1 and L_(1−s) requires Re(s)<0, so their direct sum is never trace class in the infinite-prime limit; the Hilbert–Schmidt regularization domains also have empty intersection |
| Full-shift prime symbols overcount mixed Euler factors | 1 | 25C | The product 6 has two ordered words (2,3) and (3,2), but coefficient 1 in the Euler product |
| Positive diagonal prime transfer has no intrinsic functional equation or zero selectivity | 1 | 25A | Its Euler trace is determined only by positive prime magnitudes, so reflection/zero behavior is not derived and the same identity holds for false generalized-prime systems |
| Random centering has no deterministic prime-drift consequence | 1 | 26A | The variance estimate uses E[X_p]=0 to replace the divergent deterministic first-order prime sum by sum_p p^(-2 sigma); fixing phases at 1 removes that cancellation, so the random nonvanishing proof gives no bound for the deterministic Euler product |
| Bohr mean-square convergence does not imply pointwise Euler-log convergence | 1 | 27A | The Besicovitch seminorm averages over vertical height and ignores exceptional points; at t=0, Σ_p p^(−σ) diverges for 1/2<σ<1, so the deterministic prime logarithm has no pointwise locally uniform limit there |
| Prime-density subtraction exposes a square-root-scale prime-counting remainder | 1 | 28A, 28C | Exact Stieltjes summation gives R_X(s)=X^(−s)E(X)+s∫E(u)u^(−s−1)du; convergence for σ>1/2 follows from E=O(x^(1/2+ε)), a target-strength estimate, while the floor identity alone gives only an O(x) fractional-part bound |
| Haar mean-square control does not extend uniformly to the identity residue | 1 | 29A, 29C | On the finite CRT quotient of size M, normalized Haar L² has point-evaluation norm exactly √M; this diverges under exhaustion, and the identity has all phases 1 with divergent prime-log sum for 1/2<σ<1 |
| TENSOR-SOBOLEV-PINNING-ENERGY-TRADEOFF | 1 | 31A | In the product first-difference Sobolev family with local weights p^alpha, bounded identity evaluation requires alpha>3, while the prime-character Euler energy can be summable at Re(s)=sigma<1 only if alpha<2sigma+1<3; the two requirements are incompatible |
| Uniform identity evaluation versus bounded prime-polynomial energy | 1 | 32A | The exact identity value is sum_(p<=X) p^(-sigma) -> infinity, so Hilbert duality forces the norm to diverge whenever evaluation norms are uniformly bounded; this covers arbitrary non-diagonal metrics but not direct arithmetic estimates. |
| Rare identity-cylinder concentration is invisible to unconditional Haar variance | 1 | 33A | The conditional mean is at least c*sum_(3<=p<=X)p^(-sigma) while conditional variance is bounded, but the conditioning event has exact mass e^(-J theta(X)); unconditional second moments do not control its reweighting. |
| Prime-zeta Möbius inversion needs a zero-controlled logarithm beyond absolute convergence | 1 | 33B | The k=1 term is log zeta(s); continuing one analytic branch through the strip requires controlling zeros, while local branches do not give the claimed global identity. |
| Positive Fejér smoothing preserves the divergent prime identity sum | 1 | 33C | Every nontrivial one-dimensional Fejér multiplier at frequency 1 is at least 1/2, so convolution leaves at least half of the divergent sum; zero multiplier deletes that prime mode. |
| Primorial CRT determinants and their Jacobi-theta dual quotients reproduce the rare-cylinder normalizer exactly but do not independently evaluate it | 2 | 34A, 34B | The determinant and modular scale both contain N_X=product_(p<=X)p; theta transformation returns the same input scale |
| KL/Hoeffding rare-cylinder allowance is asymptotically too loose | 1 | 35A | For fixed sigma>1/2, the bound is O(sqrt(X)) while the conditional mean is O(X^(1-sigma)/log X) (or O(log log X) at sigma=1); this kills this entropy estimate, not other possible conditioning inequalities |
| Scalar relative-entropy envelope asymptotically saturates the coordinatewise maximum | 1 | 36A | For D=J theta(X), the admissible product-uniform arc law of width exp(-D/pi(X)) gives B_X(D) >= sinc(pi exp(-D/pi(X))) sum_(p<=X)p^(-sigma), so 0<=S_X-B_X(D)->0; the actual CRT cylinder mean is S_X-C_X with C_X->C>0. Exact for this scalar KL envelope; not a no-go for prime-sensitive constraints |

| Primewise relative-entropy caps leave a fixed local-Gibbs slack | 1 | 37A | With beta(d)=I1(lambda)/I0(lambda), lambda I1/I0-log I0=d, the product bound U_X=sum_(p<=X)p^(-sigma)beta(Jlog p) exceeds the exact cylinder mean by Delta_X=sum p^(-sigma)(beta(Jlog p)-sinc(2pi/p^J)); each term is positive and Delta_X converges to Delta>0 since it is bounded by a constant times sum p^(-(sigma+2J)). Exact for this primewise relaxation; no general no-go |

| Degree-two CRT pair moments prescribe rather than derive the local mean | 1 | 38A | For product laws with at least three positive target means, imposing x_p x_q=m_p m_q for all distinct pairs forces x_p=±m_p with one common sign; maximizing a positive weighted sum selects +. The constraints are the exact CRT moments themselves, so this is a finite algebraic encoding, not an independent arithmetic estimate. |

| Chebyshev factorial cocycle is an exact divisor-sum repackaging | 1 | 39A | The first difference of sum_d Lambda(d) floor(X/d) is log X, so the lattice total is log(X!); no independent prime estimate remains |
| Shifted divisibility Gram keeps pair weights rather than the Weil one-point prime samples | 1 | 39A | CRT expansion gives Lambda(d)Lambda(e) times simultaneous divisibility counts; it does not produce the linear Lambda(n) coefficient at log n in Q |
| Root-of-unity filtering leaves divisor-pair correlations | 1 | 39B | Exact finite Fourier orthogonality filters congruences but retains two divisor indices, not a one-index prime atom |
| Finite Dirichlet ratio-lag support cannot equal the full Weil prime-power atomic distribution | 1 | 40A | A finite coefficient support gives only finitely many logarithmic ratios, while the Weil prime distribution has nonzero atoms at arbitrarily large prime logs; this rules out exact finite identification, not weak limits or inequalities |
| Reused random Euler martingale fails the canonical false-world filter | 1 | 39C | The standard random-phase nonvanishing proof is already Pass 26 and survives if phases are externally attached to a bad-zero system |
| Scattering unitarity does not locate scattering zeros | 1 | 41A | Self-adjointness gives unitary scattering on Re(s)=1/2, but zeta zeros map to scattering zeros at Re(s)=(Re(rho)+1)/2; reciprocal symmetry and boundary unitarity do not force the latter line to be 3/4 |

## Pass 1 — Frobenius moments and prime-log phases

### 1. Operator used

**TOY-FIRST.** I compared the finite-field Frobenius moment construction in an explicit small example with a candidate built from integer prime-power logarithms, then tested whether the same positive mechanism distinguishes the supplied true and false toy worlds.

### 2. Generate three candidates

**1A — Prime-log phase Toeplitz Gram.** For cutoff X, let P_X be the prime powers p^k <= X, weight w_(p^k) = log(p)/sqrt(p^k), and phase theta_(p^k) = log(p^k) modulo 2 pi. Define m_l(X) = sum_(p^k in P_X) w_(p^k) exp(i*l*theta_(p^k)) and T[j,k] = m_(j-k). Claim: every finite T is positive semidefinite.

**1B — Ihara determinant of the truncated divisibility graph.** Let G_X have vertices 1,...,X and edges n <-> n*p for prime p whenever n*p <= X; let B_X be its nonbacktracking directed-edge matrix. Claim: primitive cycles of G_X encode exactly prime powers, so det(I-u*B_X)^(-1) gives an integer Euler product with a self-adjoint graph realization.

**1C — Reflection-paired Euler compound-Poisson law.** For finite prime cutoff X and 0 < sigma < 1, let independent K_p have probabilities Pr(K_p=k)=(1-p^(-sigma))*p^(-k*sigma), and set S_(sigma,X)=sum_(p<=X) K_p*log(p); pair its law with the reflected law at 1-sigma. Claim: this positive finite law makes s <-> 1-s symmetry intrinsic while retaining local Euler factors.

### 3. Fast kill

- **1A:** It passes initial screening: it is concrete, quadratic, and has an independent exact positivity proof. Develop it and apply the false-world tests.
- **1B:** Killed by the exact X=6 counterexample below: G_6 contains a primitive four-cycle from commuting factors 2 and 3, so its primitive cycles are not just prime-power labels.
- **1C:** Killed at the symmetry step: averaging a law with its reflection imposes symmetry by construction but does not establish the completed zeta identity; the same averaging works for bad Beurling systems.

### 4. Develop strongest initial survivor: 1A

For any coefficient vector c=(c_0,...,c_N),

```text
c* T_(X,N) c = sum_(p^k<=X) w_(p^k) * |sum_(j=0)^N c_j exp(-i*j*theta_(p^k))|^2 >= 0.
```

Thus finite PSD is exact and unconditional. For sigma>1, the untruncated prime-power moments have the absolutely convergent identity

```text
sum_(p,k) log(p)/p^(k*sigma) * exp(i*l*k*log(p)) = -zeta'(sigma-i*l)/zeta(sigma-i*l).
```

At sigma=1/2 this pass uses only a finite cutoff and asserts no convergence of the displayed infinite series. The entries of T use pairwise phase differences log(n/m) modulo 2 pi, whereas the normalized Weil form samples the single locations g(log(p^k)) and also includes pole and archimedean terms. No map h -> c giving Q(h)=c*Tc, or a controlled error for that identity, has been derived.

**Finite-field toy calculation.** For E: y^2=x^3-x over F_5, direct counting gives #E(F_5)=8: affine point counts at x=0,1,2,3,4 are 1,1,2,2,1, followed by the point at infinity. Hence the Frobenius trace is a_1=5+1-8=-2 and its polynomial is T^2+2T+5, with roots -1 +/- 2i of modulus sqrt(5). The normalized roots lambda_+/-=(-1 +/- 2i)/sqrt(5) lie on the unit circle. The moments m_l=lambda_+^l+lambda_-^l give a PSD Toeplitz matrix because it is the Gram matrix of the two unit-circle phase vectors. This toy succeeds, but the Frobenius roots are already inputs; it supplies no integer-side selection principle.

**Integer numerical check.** Take X=13, sigma=1/2, and N=2. The supported prime powers are 2,3,4,5,7,8,9,11,13. At 60-digit working precision:

```text
m_0 = 4.971884398070193937828675704749430752067887456289504486
m_1 = -1.030661770203619932143678015270407101867076453996192128
      + 4.010359455835812170396915505234405405877119621574523873 i
m_2 = -1.755441710862021717159078913017956837998347620971820967
      - 1.363447295724624685344658875362538900441189745000787149 i

leading principal minors:
  Delta_1 = 4.97188439807019393782867570474943075206788746
  Delta_2 = 7.57438781820284372726238504128562689625085306
  Delta_3 = 3.12915557634025176891832128318790371604033710
```

The imaginary residuals in the high-precision minor calculation are below 7e-61. The values agree with the exact Gram proof. This computes the candidate matrix only, not Q or Xi.

### 5. Source audit

The sign comes only from the explicit sum of squares, not from a hypothesis equivalent to T. That is independent positivity. But the matrix is positive for arbitrary nonnegative weights, independent of zero locations, and no identity connects it to the complete Weil form. The finite-field example uses Frobenius roots as data rather than discovering them.

### 6. Discrimination test

| Toy world | Result | Exact step |
|---|---|---|
| (a) Curves over finite fields | **PSD analogue succeeds; no transfer** | Normalize Frobenius eigenvalues to the unit circle and use their positive atomic measure. The example gives a PSD Gram, but its eigenvalues are inputs. |
| (b) Ihara zeta of Ramanujan graphs | **PSD analogue succeeds; no Ramanujan conclusion** | A positive spectral measure gives a PSD moment Gram. PSD alone does not imply the Ramanujan spectral-support bound or identify an arithmetic zeta determinant. |
| (c) Random Euler products with independent uniform phases | **Succeeds trivially** | Expectations of phase monomials form Gram matrices. Positivity is an L2 norm and is independent of zero-free information. |
| (d) Davenport-Heilbronn function | **Prescribed construction unavailable; repaired version is nondiscriminating** | No Euler product supplies the required nonnegative prime-power weights. Replacing them by absolute Dirichlet coefficients restores a positive Gram but leaves positivity unrelated to the off-line zeros. |
| (e) Beurling systems with regular integer counts and bad prime counts | **Fails discrimination decisively** | Replace p^k and Lambda(p^k) by generalized prime powers and their nonnegative generalized von Mangoldt weights. The same Gram identity remains PSD even in the false Beurling world, so it does not reject the off-line-zero example. |

The mandatory discrimination test fails: this positive moment Gram works on the false Beurling world too.

### 7. Verdict

**DEAD.** The exact failure is the Beurling test: T is PSD for every positive atomic measure, including one attached to a generalized-prime system with off-line zeros. Also, there is no proved identity or controlled inequality between this matrix and Q. The PSD lemma is standard and too broad to constrain zero locations.

### 8. New-idea check

**Not new.** Positive Toeplitz moment matrices are the standard Gram/moment construction; see [Encyclopedia of Mathematics: Moment matrix](https://encyclopediaofmath.org/wiki/Moment_matrix). Archive precedents include `quadratic_positivity_search.md` Passes 8, 9, and 15 (character, Haar-phase, and primitive-residue Gram constructions); `RH_positive_search.md` entries EG662 (positive multiplicative Levy jump measure) and EG677 (positive multiplicative moment cone); and `OPERATOR_SUMMARY.md` Candidate EL (arithmetic Toeplitz-Hankel operator). The graph-determinant analogy is the classical Ihara/Hashimoto construction; see [Hashimoto, On zeta and L-functions of finite graphs](https://doi.org/10.1142/S0129167X90000204). This pass contributes the explicit Beurling false-world kill and X=13 diagnostic, not a new positivity method.

### 9. Lesson

Do not treat moment-matrix positivity as arithmetic selectivity. Require an exact identity or controlled inequality that preserves the one-index von-Mangoldt samples and rejects a Beurling false world.

### 10. Seed

Can an alternating exterior-algebra weight cancel the first spurious commuting-prime square in the divisibility graph while preserving a positive trace on the true finite-field and Ramanujan-graph analogues? Next operator: **SPECIALIZE**, starting with the exact 1,2,3,6 square.

## Pass 2 — The 2-by-3 commuting square and cubical Hodge energy

### 1. Operator used

**SPECIALIZE.** Restrict to the first square in the divisibility graph, on vertices 1, 2, 3, 6. It is created by the commuting prime steps 2 and 3.

### 2. Generate three candidates

**2A — Prime-exponent cubical Hodge complex.** For cutoff X, use exponent vectors a=(a_p) with product n(a)=product_p p^(a_p)<=X as vertices. Include a k-cell whenever increasing any chosen k distinct prime exponents by one still leaves the top product <=X. Put the standard Euclidean boundary maps on this finite cubical complex and its positive Hodge Laplacians Delta_k. Claim: the heat supertrace sum_k (-1)^k Tr(exp(-t Delta_k)) retains an Euler-product prime-power signal after filling commuting-prime squares.

**2B — Fermionic prime-step Dirac operator.** On the exterior algebra over one mode for each prime p<=X, let c_p^* be exterior creation and combine it with the integer shift U_(log p), for example D_X=sum_p sqrt(log(p)/p) c_p^* U_(log p). Claim: anticommutation cancels the spurious 2-by-3 square while a positive Dirac square retains prime channels and creates all prime-power lengths.

**2C — Mobius divisor-boundary energy.** For each n<=X, take the divisor-indexed vector b_n(d)=mu(d)log(n/d) for d|n and its squared norm, while the all-ones component is sum_(d|n)mu(d)log(n/d)=Lambda(n). Claim: the divisor boundary produces the exact prime-power trace and its Hodge norm is independently positive with a controllable scalar cost.

### 3. Fast kill

- **2A:** Keep for development: the finite cubical complex and positive Hodge form are explicit, and the 1,2,3,6 square is filled exactly.
- **2B:** Killed exactly at repeated powers: c_p^* c_p^*=0, so this one-mode fermionic construction annihilates p^2, p^3, and every repeated p-channel that the Euler product requires.
- **2C:** Killed by the known divisor-lift scalar obstruction: its positive norm squares all divisor components, while Lambda is recovered only by their signed sum; this repeats quadratic-search passes 12-14.

### 4. Develop strongest initial survivor: 2A

For X=6 the prime coordinates are 2 and 3. Order the vertices as (1,2,3,6), the oriented edges as (1->2, 2->6, 1->3, 3->6), and the square cell with boundary e_12+e_26-e_13-e_36. The boundary matrices are

```text
B1 = [ -1   0  -1   0 ]       B2 = [  1 ]
     [  1  -1   0   0 ]            [  1 ]
     [  0   0   1  -1 ]            [ -1 ]
     [  0   1   0   1 ]            [ -1 ]

B1*B2 = 0
Delta_1 = B1^T*B1 + B2*B2^T
        = [ 3   0   0  -1 ]
          [ 0   3  -1   0 ]
          [ 0  -1   3   0 ]
          [-1   0   0   3 ]
Spec(Delta_1) = {2,2,4,4}
Spec(Delta_0) = {0,2,2,4};  Spec(Delta_2) = {4}
chi = #vertices - #edges + #faces = 4-4+1 = 1.
```

These are exact integer-matrix calculations; no floating-point precision is involved. The graph's one-dimensional cycle is now the boundary of a 2-cell, and the first homology vanishes. Thus the square-filling part works exactly.

The full cutoff complex K_X is a union of unit cubes in the nonnegative prime-exponent orthant. Its vertex set is downward closed: if product_p p^(a_p)<=X and 0<=b_p<=a_p coordinatewise, then product_p p^(b_p)<=X. More strongly, if a point x lies in an included cube, its coordinatewise contraction t*x, 0<=t<=1, lies in an included cube because the top exponent corner can only decrease. Therefore K_X is star-shaped about the zero exponent vector and contractible. Its homology is H_0=Z and H_k=0 for k>0, so chi(K_X)=1 for every X.

For any finite cubical chain complex, the alternating heat trace of its Hodge Laplacians is the Euler characteristic: nonzero exact/coexact eigenmodes cancel in adjacent degrees, leaving the harmonic dimensions. Hence

```text
sum_k (-1)^k Tr(exp(-t*Delta_k)) = chi(K_X) = 1, for every t>=0 and every X.
```

This disproves 2A's proposed prime-trace claim for this observable. Its positivity is real, but its alternating Hodge supertrace is topological and constant. Individual Laplacian spectra may retain other information; this calculation is not a no-go for every operator on K_X.

### 5. Source audit

The quadratic energy is independently positive because <u,Delta_k u>=||d_k u||^2+||d_(k-1)^*u||^2. Filling the 2,3 square follows from the exact commutation of multiplication by 2 and 3. The candidate fails when the chosen Euler supertrace is forced by contractibility to equal 1, not through an assumption equivalent to T. The positivity is not tied to von Mangoldt weights or zero locations.

### 6. Discrimination test

| Toy world | Result | Exact step |
|---|---|---|
| (a) Curves over finite fields | **Fails to transfer the true spectral data** | Curve zeta data come from Frobenius on cohomology; the prime-exponent cubical complex has contractible homology and supertrace 1, not the Frobenius characteristic polynomial. |
| (b) Ihara zeta of Ramanujan graphs | **Fills only the commuting square; no Ramanujan bound** | The 1,2,3,6 square is a commuting-coordinate cell. General graph prime cycles are nonbacktracking cycles and need not be boundaries of such cells; the construction yields no spectral-support estimate. |
| (c) Random Euler products with independent uniform phases | **Fails to encode the true toy output** | The down-closed exponent complex and its supertrace are unchanged by the independent phases; the output remains 1 rather than recording the random Euler-product zeros. |
| (d) Davenport-Heilbronn function | **Arithmetic complex unavailable** | There is no Euler-product prime coordinate set from which to form the prescribed cubical cells; an arbitrary replacement complex still has no canonical DH determinant. |
| (e) Beurling systems with regular integer counts and bad prime counts | **Fails discrimination** | Replacing ordinary primes by generalized primes gives the same down-closed exponent geometry and the same supertrace 1, even when the associated system has off-line zeros. |

The candidate does not pass the mandatory test: it does not reproduce the spectra in the true worlds and its output is insensitive to the false Beurling zero locations.

### 7. Verdict

**DEAD.** The exact claimed observable is constant: for this finite downward-closed prime-exponent cubical complex, chi(K_X)=1 and the Hodge heat supertrace equals 1 for every cutoff and heat time. It therefore cannot be the proposed prime-power trace. This is candidate-specific; no conclusion is drawn about determinants or non-alternating spectral data of K_X.

### 8. New-idea check

The archive search found no prior prime-exponent cubical Hodge-complex candidate. The ingredients are standard: finite cubical chain complexes, Hodge Laplacians, and Euler-characteristic supertraces. The graph-zeta side of the analogy is classical Ihara/Hashimoto theory; see [Hashimoto, On zeta and L-functions of finite graphs](https://doi.org/10.1142/S0129167X90000204). The specific cutoff complex and its star-shaped contraction are a new project diagnostic in this ledger, not a claim of literature novelty or a theorem about zeta zeros.

### 9. Lesson

Filling commuting-factor cycles can remove a finite graph homology class, but if the full prime-exponent cutoff is downward closed then the Euler supertrace collapses to 1; any next cell construction must preserve a nontrivial arithmetic grading in the observable rather than use ordinary Euler characteristic alone.

### 10. Seed

Can a prime-length-graded cellular chain complex retain the logarithmic length of each prime-power edge while using cells only to cancel commuting-factor squares? Next operator: **GENERALIZE**.


## Pass 3 — A dimension-fugacity deformation of the prime-exponent complex

### 1. Operator used

**GENERALIZE.** Replace the single cutoff complex by a two-parameter family: vary the finite prime set, the exponent cap, and a cell-dimension fugacity, then ask whether the family keeps more than the ordinary Euler characteristic while retaining an arithmetic trace.

### 2. Generate three candidates

**3A — Dimension-fugacity prime-exponent partition function.** For a finite prime set \(P\), exponent cap \(K\), and complex \(s,z\), use the product of path cells with vertex weight \(p^{-ks}\) and edge weight \(-z p^{-ks}\). Its explicit generating function is \(Z_{P,K}(s,z)=\prod_{p\in P}[1+(1-z)\sum_{k=1}^{K}p^{-ks}]\). Claim: the cell grading supplies a positive, symmetry-compatible spectral object whose logarithmic derivative continues to the completed arithmetic spectrum.

**3B — Finite-prime diagonal transfer determinant.** On one coordinate per prime in finite P, let T_P(s)=diag(p^{-s}) and define Z_P(s)=det(I-T_P(s))^{-1}=product_{p in P}(1-p^{-s})^{-1}; prime powers arise by expanding the logarithm. Claim: a canonical determinant completion of these local blocks is invariant under s -> 1-s and exposes its zeros spectrally.

**3C — Length-weighted cubical Euler character.** On the product of prime-exponent paths, assign each cell the weight \(n(c)^{-s}\), where \(n(c)\) is the product of its top exponents' prime powers, and form \(E_{P,K}(s)=\sum_c(-1)^{\dim c}n(c)^{-s}\). Claim: retaining logarithmic length inside the signed Hodge trace avoids the unweighted Euler-characteristic collapse and yields prime-power data.

### 3. Fast kill

- **3A:** Keep for full audit because its finite identity and limiting logarithmic derivative are explicit and cheaply checkable; the central selectivity claim must face the Beurling false world.
- **3B:** Killed immediately: the finite Euler product has no reflection symmetry; for \(P=\{2\}\), \(Z_2(2)=4/3\) but \(Z_2(-1)=-1\). Adding a completion that repairs this is extra input, not a consequence of the finite transfer determinant.
- **3C:** Killed by exact local cancellation: for each prime, the vertex contribution \(1+\sum_{k=1}^Kp^{-ks}\) minus the edge contribution \(\sum_{k=1}^Kp^{-ks}\) is exactly 1, so \(E_{P,K}(s)=1\) for every finite \(P,K,s\).

### 4. Develop strongest initial survivor: 3A

For each \(p\in P\), take the path with vertices \(v_0,\ldots,v_K\) and edges \(e_1,\ldots,e_K\), where \(e_k\) joins exponents \(k-1\) and \(k\). Give a vertex at exponent \(k\) weight \(p^{-ks}\), and an edge with top exponent \(k\) weight \(-z p^{-ks}\). Product cells have multiplicative weights, so their total generating function factors prime by prime:

\[
Z_{P,K}(s,z)=\prod_{p\in P}\left(1+\sum_{k=1}^Kp^{-ks}-z\sum_{k=1}^Kp^{-ks}\right)
=\prod_{p\in P}\left(1+(1-z)\sum_{k=1}^Kp^{-ks}\right).
\]

At \(z=1\), this is the ordinary alternating cell sum and equals 1. At \(z=0\), it selects vertices and becomes a finite product of truncated geometric series. Define \(D_{P,K}(s)=-\partial_s\log Z_{P,K}(s,0)\). Direct differentiation gives the exact formula

\[
D_{P,K}(s)=\sum_{p\in P}\log p\;\frac{\sum_{k=1}^K k p^{-ks}}{\sum_{k=0}^Kp^{-ks}}.
\]

For fixed finite \(P\) and real \(s>0\), sending \(K\to\infty\) gives \(Z_{P,\infty}(s,0)=\prod_{p\in P}(1-p^{-s})^{-1}\) and

\[
D_{P,\infty}(s)=\sum_{p\in P}\frac{\log p}{p^s-1}.
\]

For \(\Re s>1\), then letting \(P\) increase through all primes is justified by absolute convergence and yields

\[
\sum_p\frac{\log p}{p^s-1}=\sum_{p}\sum_{k\ge1}\frac{\log p}{p^{ks}}=-\frac{\zeta'(s)}{\zeta(s)}.
\]

This is an exact multiplicative-to-additive bridge in its ordinary Euler-product half-plane. It does not define an operator, a positive-semidefinite quadratic form, or an analytic continuation to the critical strip. The parameter choice \(z=0\) discards all positive-dimensional cells; no intrinsic mechanism selects it as a spectral trace.

**Explicit high-precision check.** For \(P=\{2,3,5,7\}\), \(s=2\), and \(K=40\), 60-digit arithmetic gives:

```text
Z_{P,40}(2,0) = 1.595052083333333333333333003484293496919731395174456076
D_{P,40}(2)  = 0.4759753040602353573615369725556272307119664139795081805

K=infinity, fixed P:
Z_{P,infinity}(2,0) = 1.595052083333333333333333333333333333333333333333333333
D_{P,infinity}(2)   = 0.4759753040602353573615428494741986317871090194754057793

absolute K=40 errors:
|delta Z| = 3.29849039836414e-25
|delta D| = 5.87691857140108e-24
```

These errors are consistent with the omitted geometric tails and validate the finite differentiation identity. They do not test or imply a zero-location statement.

### 5. Source audit

For real \(s>0\), the scalar factors at \(z=0\) are sums of positive terms, so scalar positivity is independent of any zero data. The logarithmic derivative identity follows only from differentiating the finite geometric factors and, in the infinite-prime limit, from the absolutely convergent Euler product for \(\Re s>1\); no target positivity is assumed. But the positivity is not a positive-semidefinite form, and the computation imports the local prime factors as input. The special fugacity \(z=0\) is a vertex projection, not a theorem about the chain complex.

### 6. Discrimination test

| Toy world | Result | Exact step |
|---|---|---|
| (a) Curves over finite fields | **Euler product reproduced; spectral theorem absent** | Replacing primes by closed points gives the formal local product, but this scalar identity supplies no Frobenius cohomology or eigenvalue bound. |
| (b) Ihara zeta of Ramanujan graphs | **Euler product reproduced; Ramanujan bound absent** | Replacing primes by primitive cycles gives the graph Euler product, but the cell weights do not construct the nonbacktracking adjacency spectrum or constrain it to the Ramanujan interval. |
| (c) Random Euler products with independent uniform phases | **Fails to encode the random phases** | The defined \(Z_{P,K}\) depends only on positive weights \(p^{-ks}\); replacing local factors by random phases changes the toy function but leaves this object unchanged unless phases are inserted as new input. |
| (d) Davenport-Heilbronn function | **Not defined from its data** | This construction requires a local Euler factor for each prime; the prescribed function has no Euler product from which the path weights can be obtained. |
| (e) Beurling systems with regular integer counts and bad prime counts | **Fails discrimination** | Replacing \(p\) by generalized primes \(q\) gives the same identity \(-\zeta_B'/\zeta_B(s)=\sum_q\sum_{k\ge1}(\log q)q^{-ks}\) wherever the Beurling Euler product converges; the algebra does not detect that the model has off-line zeros. |

The mandatory test fails at (e): the exact bridge and scalar positivity survive in a false zero-geometry model.

### 7. Verdict

**DEAD.** The computation proves only the standard logarithmic derivative of a finite or absolutely convergent Euler product. The family has no reflection symmetry (candidate 3B gives an explicit two-value counterexample), no positive-semidefinite form, and no canonical spectral determinant equal to the completed function. Its Beurling analogue still works with bad zeros, so the construction is not discriminating.

### 8. New-idea check

The archive search found related prime-exponent cubical Hodge work in Pass 2 and an all-prime finite-adelic cubical normal-crossings proposal in `frobenius_search_v3.md`; `RH_positive_search.md` also contains KMS partition-function constructions. The exact fugacity polynomial above was not located by the targeted search, but setting its fugacity to zero simply recovers the standard finite Euler product, and its dimension-one alternating specialization repeats Pass 2's Euler-characteristic cancellation. No literature novelty is claimed.

### 9. Lesson

A grading parameter can interpolate between a trivial Euler characteristic and the ordinary Euler product, but unless a canonical operator fixes the parameter and controls a false Beurling model, it is only an Euler-product repackaging.

### 10. Seed

Can the multiplicative exponent paths be replaced by a different object whose independently defined cohomology has a nontrivial length-graded trace, rather than selecting vertices by a free fugacity? Next operator: **CHANGE-THE-OBJECT**.


## Pass 4 — Prime-log point interactions and a positive scattering Hamiltonian

### 1. Operator used

**CHANGE-THE-OBJECT.** Replace the prime-exponent cell complex by a finite-interval Schrödinger operator whose interaction sites are the additive logarithms of primes. Its positive energy and reflection symmetry are intrinsic to the operator; its local scattering amplitudes are then compared with Euler factors.

### 2. Generate three candidates

**4A — Even prime-log delta-barrier Hamiltonian.** For cutoff P, let L=2 log P and define on L²([-L,L]) with Dirichlet endpoints \(H_P=-d²/du²+\sum_{p\le P}(\log p)[\delta(u-\log p)+\delta(u+\log p)]\). Claim: its positive spectral determinant, with k² as spectral parameter, has prime Euler amplitudes and converges to the completed arithmetic determinant.

**4B — Prime-length quantum graph.** Build a compact metric graph with loop lengths log p and nonnegative self-adjoint Laplacian; use its secular determinant as the arithmetic object. Claim: repetitions of each loop generate p^k, while time reversal supplies the functional-equation reflection and positivity supplies real spectral parameters.

**4C — Xi-centered de Branges pair.** Let F(z)=Xi(1/2+iz), define E(z)=F(z)-iF'(z), and form its de Branges kernel. Claim: the Hermite-Biehler condition on E gives a positive space whose multiplication spectrum is exactly the zero ordinates.

### 3. Fast kill

- **4A:** Keep for a concrete local scattering calculation: positivity and reflection follow from the form and parity, but the natural point-scatter coefficient must match the Euler factor without fitting.
- **4B:** Killed as an archive repeat: Candidate DQ2 already assigns prime lengths to a positive quantum graph and records the missing canonical scattering amplitudes, gamma phase, and Xi determinant.
- **4C:** Killed by source audit: the Xi-derived Hermite-Biehler inequality is the global zero-location condition in a different package; Candidate DL/EM already records this exact circular-strength requirement.

### 4. Develop strongest initial survivor: 4A

For a finite prime set P, let the quadratic form be

\[
q_P[f]=\int_{-L}^{L}|f'(u)|²\,du+\sum_{p\le P}(\log p)(|f(\log p)|²+|f(-\log p)|²),\qquad f\in H_0^1([-L,L]).
\]

Point evaluation is continuous on the one-dimensional form domain, so this defines a self-adjoint operator with compact resolvent. The form is nonnegative. Because the potential is even, reflection \(Rf(u)=f(-u)\) commutes with \(H_P\). Thus its finite-interval spectral determinant depends on \(k²\) and is invariant under \(k\mapsto-k\), the centered coordinate reflection corresponding to \(s=1/2+ik\mapsto1-s\).

The exact local scattering calculation exposes the bridge mismatch. For one delta interaction of strength \(g\) at the origin, continuity and the derivative jump \(\psi'(0^+)-\psi'(0^-)=g\psi(0)\) give the reflection coefficient

\[
r_g(k)=\frac{g}{2ik-g},\qquad |r_g(k)|=\frac{g}{\sqrt{g²+4k²}}.
\]

With the candidate's arithmetic choice \(g=\log p\), the modulus varies with k. On the critical line, the elementary Euler amplitude \(p^{-s}\), for \(s=1/2+ik\), has constant modulus \(p^{-1/2}\). Therefore this constant-strength delta-scatter rule cannot equal the local Euler amplitude for all k. For p=2 and k=1, the two moduli are respectively 0.3274647049603148984872917205221324830176273397436081571 and 0.707106..., a discrepancy before any infinite-cutoff issue. The exact values below give the scattering coefficient, so the rounded magnitude is not being used as a proof.

**Finite spectral computation.** Take P={2}, a=log 2, L=2a, and put barriers of strength g=a at u=±a on [-2a,2a]. The ground state is even. On [0,a] it is proportional to cos(ku); across u=a its derivative jumps by gψ(a); imposing ψ(2a)=0 gives

\[
F(k)=\cos(ka)\cos(ka)+\left[-\sin(ka)+\frac{g}{k}\cos(ka)\right]\sin(ka)=0.
\]

Bisection at 70-digit working precision gives

```text
first positive k = 1.3185045222233088861012935993223130565796001326814152850358222198
lambda_1 = k^2 = 1.7384541751233160363045439801240523188571418162642523293835414994
free lambda_1 = 1.2838930284514887740600721290868583771511576337183508977315654977
barrier-induced shift = 0.454561146671827262244471851037193941705984183
characteristic residual = 1.35835186182e-71
```

At k=1, the exact one-site coefficient for p=2 is

```text
r_2(1) = -0.1072331329947460848820667663015190800167
          - 0.3094094183810137080153323051893628921596 i
|r_2(1)| = 0.3274647049603148984872917205221324830176273397436081571
2^(-1/2) = 0.7071067811865475244008443621048490392848
```

The exact modulus is \(\log 2/\sqrt{(\log 2)^2+4}\); use that expression for comparisons. The eigenvalue calculation verifies a concrete positive finite operator and its parity symmetry; it gives no identification with the target determinant.

### 5. Source audit

Positivity comes from the closed quadratic form, and reflection comes from the explicitly even point potential; neither uses zero locations. The amplitude mismatch follows from the point-interaction jump condition, not from a conjectural estimate. This is a local no-match result for the fixed delta strengths \(g_p=\log p\) and this standard scattering normalization; energy-dependent boundary couplings or additional channels are not excluded. No Xi identity or completion is supplied.

### 6. Discrimination test

| Toy world | Result | Exact step |
|---|---|---|
| (a) Curves over finite fields | **Fails spectral transfer** | Replacing prime sites by closed-point log degrees still gives a positive even Schrödinger operator, but its real eigenvalues do not encode Frobenius eigenvalues or the curve-zeta zero data. |
| (b) Ihara zeta of Ramanujan graphs | **Fails the Ramanujan test** | Replacing prime sites by primitive-cycle lengths gives a positive operator, but parity and positivity do not impose the graph adjacency spectral bound; no Ihara determinant identity follows. |
| (c) Random Euler products with independent uniform phases | **Fails phase sensitivity** | The real positive couplings \(g_p=\log p\) do not depend on the independent phases; inserting phase-valued couplings generally destroys self-adjointness and the positive form. |
| (d) Davenport-Heilbronn function | **Unavailable from its data** | The construction takes prime sites and strengths from an Euler product; the prescribed function has no such canonical prime data. |
| (e) Beurling systems with regular integer counts and bad prime counts | **Fails discrimination** | Replacing p by generalized primes q leaves the point-interaction form nonnegative and even, hence its spectral parameter remains real regardless of the Beurling zeta's off-line zeros; no determinant equality links the two. |

The construction does not distinguish the true worlds from the false Beurling world: its positivity and symmetric spectrum are properties of the chosen Hamiltonian, not of the associated zeta zeros.

### 7. Verdict

**DEAD.** The arithmetic interaction locations provide additive log-prime sites, but the natural point-scatter amplitude \((\log p)/\sqrt{(\log p)^2+4k^2}\) is not the Euler modulus \(p^{-1/2}\); the finite Hamiltonian's positive spectrum survives unchanged in a false Beurling analogue. The object does have exact positivity and a reflection symmetry, but no canonical determinant identity with Xi or zero correspondence.

### 8. New-idea check

**Not new at the structural level.** The archive already contains Candidate EY (an even prime-power point-interaction Dirac operator with positive square and eta phase), Candidate FC (an arithmetic limit-point Weyl system), Candidate DQ2 (a positive prime-length quantum graph), and `RH_positive_search.md`'s logarithmic-coordinate Schrödinger potential. This pass is only a simplified second-order finite-interval diagnostic; its exact double-delta eigenvalue is not claimed as a new RH result. Candidate 4C repeats Candidates DL and EM.

### 9. Lesson

An even positive operator can supply spectral reality and reflection cheaply, but prime locations alone do not fix the energy-dependent scattering amplitudes or distinguish a false Euler system; derive the local amplitude identity before scaling up.

### 10. Seed

Can a positivity theorem imported from Hodge-Riemann/Lorentzian theory force the necessary amplitude relation from a canonical arithmetic graded object, rather than assigning a positive potential? Next operator: **IMPORT**.


## Pass 5 — Lorentzian prime polynomials and the local Euler factor

### 1. Operator used

**IMPORT.** Import Lorentzian-polynomial/Hodge–Riemann signature into the finite prime-local generating factors. The test is whether the independently available one-positive-direction Hessian can coexist with the exact geometric series that encodes every prime power.

### 2. Generate three candidates

**5A — Lorentzian binomial prime-power polynomial.** For finite primes P and real s>1, write q_p=p^{-s} and set \(F_{P,K}(y,(x_p))=\prod_{p\in P}(y+q_px_p)^K\). Products of nonnegative linear forms give an intrinsically Lorentzian polynomial, and each local factor has terms for powers p^k. Claim: its normalized logarithmic derivative yields the exact von Mangoldt Euler trace while its Hessian provides a canonical positive primitive form.

**5B — Uniform-matroid prime basis polynomial.** For a finite prime set P and rank r, use \(B_{P,r}(x)=\sum_{A\subseteq P,|A|=r}(\prod_{p\in A}p^{-s})\prod_{p\in A}x_p\), the weighted basis polynomial of the uniform matroid. Claim: Hodge–Riemann signature on its primitive derivative spaces supplies a scale-uniform positive quadratic form that controls the prime logarithmic derivative.

**5C — Geometric local Euler polynomial.** At each prime use \(G_{p,2}(x,y)=y²+q_pxy+q_p²x²\), the degree-two truncation of the local geometric series, and tensor the factors over p. Claim: its coefficients exactly retain p and p² while its local Hodge–Riemann Hessian remains Lorentzian.

### 3. Fast kill

- **5A:** Keep for full audit: it is genuinely Lorentzian and carries repeated-prime monomials, but its local coefficient ratios must be checked against the Euler denominator.
- **5B:** Killed immediately: every monomial uses each prime at most once, so the basis support contains no p² or higher prime powers; the matroid Hodge–Riemann theorem does not repair the missing local Euler factors.
- **5C:** Killed by the exact local Hessian: its Hessian is positive definite for every q_p>0, hence has two positive directions and is not Lorentzian.

### 4. Develop strongest initial survivor: 5A

For each finite P and integer K≥1, define

\[
F_{P,K}(s;y,(x_p))=\prod_{p\in P}(y+p^{-s}x_p)^K.
\]

For real s, this is a homogeneous polynomial with nonnegative coefficients. It is a product of nonnegative linear forms, hence Lorentzian; the imported Hodge–Riemann structure gives one positive Hessian direction and a nonpositive Hessian on the corresponding primitive subspace. This is an intrinsic signature statement about the polynomial, independent of any zero locations. For K≥2, the local expansion includes repeated powers, but its coefficient at p^k is \(\binom Kk p^{-ks}\), not the Euler coefficient \(p^{-ks}\).

Evaluate at y=x_p=1 and normalize by K. With q_p=p^{-s},

\[
D^{\rm Lor}_{P}(s):=-\frac1K\partial_s\log F_{P,K}(s;1,\mathbf1)
=\sum_{p\in P}\frac{(\log p)q_p}{1+q_p}.
\]

The exact Euler logarithmic derivative on the same finite prime set is

\[
D^{\rm Euler}_{P}(s)=\sum_{p\in P}\frac{(\log p)q_p}{1-q_p}
=\sum_{p\in P}\sum_{k\ge1}(\log p)p^{-ks}.
\]

For every q in (0,1), \(q/(1+q)\ne q/(1-q)\), and the difference is \(2q²/(1-q²)>0\). Thus the locally Lorentzian binomial factors undercount the higher-power contribution with a fixed exact discrepancy; increasing K only multiplies the same ratio before the normalization by K.

**Explicit 60-digit computation.** Take P={2,3}, s=2, so q₂=1/4 and q₃=1/9. The Hessian of \(F=(y+x_2/4)(y+x_3/9)\) in coordinates (y,x₂,x₃) is

```text
[ 2       1/4       1/9    ]
[ 1/4     0         1/36   ]
[ 1/9     1/36      0      ]

eigenvalues:
-0.03711970569969274109059925344670841201934681530246700954
 0  (exact; numerical residual 7.7e-63)
 2.03711970569969274109059925344670841201934681530246701

D_Lor = 0.248490664978800031022970947983887884079849082654325996
D_Euler= 0.3683755962701621478968163617680412357727696978479287662
gap    = 0.1198849312913621168738454137841533516929206151936027702
```

The Hessian has one positive, one negative, and one zero eigenvalue; its null vector is exactly \((-1,4,9)\). For the competing geometric p² truncation at p=2, q=1/4, the Hessian is

```text
[ 1/8   1/4 ]
[ 1/4   2   ]

eigenvalues:
0.09223908148374851694609613955168870606547357957683262964
2.03276091851625148305390386044831129393452642042316737
```

Its determinant is \(3q²=3/16>0\), so both eigenvalues are positive: the exact geometric coefficients at the first repeated prime power do not have the Lorentzian signature.

More generally, no finite polynomial A(q) can have \(qA'(q)/A(q)=q/(1-q)\) on an open interval: integrating would force \(A(q)=C/(1-q)\), which is not a polynomial. This rules out exact all-q recovery by any finite-degree scalar local polynomial with logarithmic derivative as the proposed observable; it does not rule out infinite series or multichannel/nonlocal lifts.

### 5. Source audit

The one-positive-direction signature comes from the established Lorentzian-polynomial theorem of Brändén and Huh; in this candidate it is also visible in the exact factorization into positive linear forms. The local derivative mismatch is elementary algebra and does not use the target inequality. The finite-polynomial no-go is only for scalar polynomial local factors differentiated logarithmically. The positive primitive Hessian is not the target quadratic form and is independent of zero geometry.

### 6. Discrimination test

| Toy world | Result | Exact step |
|---|---|---|
| (a) Curves over finite fields | **Signature without Frobenius control** | Replacing p by closed-point degrees gives positive Lorentzian factors, but their Hessians do not identify Frobenius eigenvalues or Weil bounds. |
| (b) Ihara zeta of Ramanujan graphs | **Signature without Ramanujan control** | Applying the same positive polynomial to primitive cycle lengths preserves Lorentzian signature but gives no adjacency-spectrum bound or Ihara determinant identity. |
| (c) Random Euler products with independent uniform phases | **Fails phase sensitivity** | Nonnegative Lorentzian coefficients require real nonnegative local weights; inserting independent complex phases leaves the Lorentzian cone, while replacing phases by moduli erases the random model. |
| (d) Davenport-Heilbronn function | **Unavailable from its data** | The polynomial is built from local Euler factors; the prescribed function has no canonical prime-factor input. |
| (e) Beurling systems with regular integer counts and bad prime counts | **Fails discrimination** | Substituting generalized primes into the same product preserves its Lorentzian signature and local positive coefficients independently of the associated zeta's off-line zeros. |

It fails the false Beurling test; importing Hodge–Riemann structure adds a robust signature but no zero-selective arithmetic constraint.

### 7. Verdict

**DEAD.** Lorentzian binomial factors provide intrinsic signature and repeated-prime monomials, but their local logarithmic derivative is \(q/(1+q)\), whereas the Euler factor requires \(q/(1-q)\). The exact geometric truncation that retains the correct first two local coefficients has a positive-definite Hessian with two positive eigenvalues, contrary to the Lorentzian signature. The surviving Lorentzian positivity also transfers unchanged to bad Beurling systems.

### 8. New-idea check

The imported result is standard: Brändén–Huh prove the one-positive-eigenvalue Hessian signature for Lorentzian polynomials ([Annals of Mathematics paper](https://annals.math.princeton.edu/2020/192-3/p04)). The archive already contains a matroid-basis-polynomial candidate in `RH_positive_search.md` (entry around line 14194), but not this exact local (q/(1+q)) versus (q/(1-q)) audit. The product of positive linear forms is a standard Lorentzian example; no literature novelty is claimed for the construction. The finite local Hessian countercheck is candidate-specific.

### 9. Lesson

An imported signature theorem can enforce positivity only after coefficient constraints are met; compare the exact prime-power local series before using global Hodge–Riemann machinery.

### 10. Seed

Can a positive multichannel lift preserve the local rational function \(q/(1-q)\) while satisfying a Lorentzian signature after projection, or does the exact local pole force a non-Lorentzian direction? Next operator: **COUNTEREXAMPLE-ANATOMY**.

## Pass 6 — Least-counterexample anatomy and superabundant records

### 1. Operator used

**COUNTEREXAMPLE-ANATOMY.** Assume the first integer violating the divisor-sum bound exists. Compress that first failure using the monotonicity of its right-hand side, then inspect exactly what the resulting record condition says about prime factors.

### 2. Generate three candidates

**6A — Tail-record reduction.** Let (A(n)=\sigma(n)/n) and (B(n)=e^\gamma\log\log n). Claim: a least violation above 5040 must be a strict record of (A) after the verified initial interval, hence a superabundant integer; this narrows the search to an exact, computable subsequence.

**6B — Exponent-vector exchange constraints.** For a record (n=\prod p_j^{a_j}), compare (A(n)) with (A(np_i/p_j)) and with integers obtained by inserting a missing smaller prime. Claim: these local swaps force consecutive prime support and nonincreasing exponents, giving a constrained exponent-vector polytope.

**6C — Local-factor defect product.** Write (A(n)=\prod_{p^a\parallel n}(1-p^{-a-1})/(1-p^{-1})) and define (D(n)=1-A(n)/B(n)). Claim: a positive decomposition of (D(n)) over local prime-power channels could certify the required strict sign on every record integer.

### 3. Fast kill

- **6A:** Develop as a known reduction, then test whether the record compression discriminates the required false worlds; the source audit already indicates it is a general order argument.
- **6B:** Killed as a new method: the exponent-ordering theorem for superabundant numbers is classical (Alaoglu–Erdős), and the exchange inequalities concern only maximization of \(\sigma(n)/n\), not spectral data.
- **6C:** Killed immediately as stated: (D(n)>0) is exactly the target inequality rewritten as a normalized defect, so a positive decomposition would have to prove the sign it assumes.

### 4. Develop strongest initial survivor: 6A

Put (A(n)=\sigma(n)/n) and (B(n)=e^\gamma\log\log n), for (n>1). A Robin violation is (A(n)\ge B(n)). The function (B(x)) is strictly increasing for (x>1), since (B'(x)=e^\gamma/(x\log x)>0).

The known least-counterexample reduction proceeds as follows. There are no violations for (5040<n\le10080), and 10080 is superabundant. Suppose (n>10080) is the least violation but is not superabundant. Then some (k<n) has (A(k)\ge A(n)). If (k<10080), superabundance of 10080 gives (A(10080)>A(k)\ge A(n)), so replace (k) by 10080. Thus one can take (10080\le k<n). Minimality gives (A(k)<B(k)), and monotonicity gives (B(k)<B(n)\le A(n)), contradicting (A(k)\ge A(n)). Therefore the least violation must be superabundant. This is the published Akbary–Friggstad reduction; the proof depends on the finite verification through 10080 and is not a new result of this pass.

**Exact finite diagnostic.** A divisor-sum sieve computed (\sigma(n)=\sum_{d\mid n}d) for every (n\le200000), retained exactly those (n) for which (A(n)) exceeds every earlier value, and then evaluated (R(n)=A(n)/B(n)) at 70-digit mpmath precision. The seven records above 5040 in this range are:

```text
n       sigma(n)  A(n)=sigma(n)/n              R(n)=A(n)/B(n)                                             1-R(n)
10080   39312     3.9                                          0.9858186119723289975647769687190581547682615683019865542  0.01418138802767100243522303128094184523173843169801344578

25200   99944     3.966031746031746031746031746031746031746031746031746032  0.9614892797752522572879453293473921590727411548640758614  0.03851072022474774271205467065260784092725884513592413864
27720   112320    4.051948051948051948051948051948051948051948051948051948  0.9783637690488437124311392575671436657967146153961730546  0.02163623095115628756886074243285633420328538460382694541
55440   232128    4.187012987012987012987012987012987012987012987012987013  0.9832539638470400010438595487943623433974571148304723524  0.01674603615295999895614045120463765660254288516952764759
110880  471744    4.254545454545454545454545454545454545454545454545454545  0.9740474293914482923581376380094565424920896636341540578  0.02595257060855170764186236199054345750791033636584594224

```

The exact record test is rational: for each listed (n), its fraction \(\sigma(n)/n\) strictly exceeds every preceding fraction in the full sieve. The decimal (R(n)) values use the irrational constant (e^\gamma\) and are diagnostics only. All seven displayed values lie below 1; this finite scan is not an estimate for unscanned integers.

The record reduction is a useful search compression but not a certificate for the sign. It tells us where a first violation would have to occur, not why (A(n)<B(n)) there. Moreover, the proof is driven by minimality plus the monotone envelope (B): the same argument works for any ordered sequence with a monotone barrier and an earlier-record statistic.

### 5. Source audit

The reduction's only inequality input is the assumed first violation together with the already checked base interval; the contradiction uses strict monotonicity of (B) and maximality of (A(k)). It does not independently estimate the Robin defect. Akbary and Friggstad, Theorem 3, state and prove that the least counterexample to Robin's inequality is superabundant: [paper PDF](https://www.cs.uleth.ca/~akbary/07-0300.pdf). The exponent-order structure in 6B is part of the classical Alaoglu–Erdős theory: [1944 paper scan](https://users.renyi.hu/~p_erdos/1944-03.pdf). Candidate 6C is circular unless its decomposition itself proves positivity without assuming (D(n)>0).

### 6. Discrimination test

| Toy world | Result | Exact step |
|---|---|---|
| (a) Curves over finite fields | **No spectral discrimination** | A least-index violation of any increasing envelope is an extremal record by the same order argument; the proof never uses Frobenius eigenvalues or their location. |
| (b) Ihara zeta of Ramanujan graphs | **No Ramanujan discrimination** | Replacing (A(n)) and (B(n)) by ordered graph statistics preserves the record lemma, while no adjacency-spectrum bound enters. |
| (c) Random Euler products with independent uniform phases | **Phase-blind** | Applying the argument to local-factor magnitudes discards the independent phases; the monotone-envelope proof has no phase variable. |
| (d) Davenport–Heilbronn function | **False-world-compatible** | The minimal-index/monotone-barrier implication is purely order-theoretic and remains valid for coefficient statistics attached to a function with off-line zeros; no Euler-product or zero-location premise appears in the proof. |
| (e) Beurling systems with regular integer counts and bad prime counts | **Fails discrimination** | Replace ordinary integers and (A(n)) by an ordered generalized-integer sequence and its multiplicative divisor ratio. The same record proof uses only monotonicity and minimality, so bad zero locations are not excluded. |

The developed result therefore fails the mandatory false-world test. It is a search reduction, not a selective positivity mechanism.

### 7. Verdict

**DEAD.** Candidate 6A is already Theorem 3 of Akbary–Friggstad and passes the same record compression in systems with no zero-location control. The finite scan only confirms a handful of ordinary integer records and cannot supply the missing sign estimate. Candidates 6B and 6C respectively import a classical abundance classification without spectral content and restate the desired sign as a defect factorization.

### 8. New-idea check

**Not new.** The least-counterexample-to-superabundant reduction is explicitly in Akbary–Friggstad, Theorem 3. The ordered-exponent structure is classical Alaoglu–Erdős. The local product for (\sigma(n)/n) is the standard multiplicativity formula, and (D(n)) is only a normalization of the target difference. The sieve and high-precision values are pass-specific finite diagnostics, not a new theorem.

### 9. Lesson

A first-failure argument that uses only a monotone barrier and an extremal-record statistic cannot discriminate zero geometry; after compressing to record integers, require a new phase-sensitive or independently evaluated constraint on their prime-exponent data.

### 10. Seed

Can an off-line zero force an integer-valued or sign-definite invariant on the prime-exponent vector of a hypothetical record integer, with a contradiction that survives the bad-Beurling test? Next operator: **IMPOSSIBILITY**.

## Pass 7 — Can positive prime counts contradict an off-line zero?

### 1. Operator used

**IMPOSSIBILITY.** Assume one zero \(\rho=\beta+i\gamma\) with (1/2<\beta<1), and try to make positivity of prime-counting increments force a contradiction. The key test is whether the chosen positive observable can remove the pole-scale main term without introducing signed weights.

### 2. Generate three candidates

**7A — Single-zero interval increment.** For (h>0), examine the explicit-formula residue (Z_\rho(X,h)=((X+h)^\rho-X^\rho)/\rho) inside \(\psi(X+h)-\psi(X)\), a nonnegative prime-power increment. Claim: an off-line zero makes this positive count negative on some long sequence of intervals.

**7B — Pole-annihilating finite difference.** Apply an (r)-th finite-difference stencil (\sum_{j=0}^r(-1)^{r-j}\binom rj\psi(X+jh)), which, for r≥2, kills every polynomial main term of degree less than r and magnifies oscillatory zero contributions. Claim: the result retains a sign forced by nonnegative prime coefficients.

**7C — Positive Mellin packet with zero at the pole.** For a nonzero smooth weight (w\ge0) on the positive axis, use (S_w(X)=\sum_n\Lambda(n)w(n/X)) and its Mellin transform (\widehat w(s)=\int_0^\infty w(u)u^{s-1}du). Claim: choose (w) so that (\widehat w(1)=0), suppressing the pole while preserving positivity of the prime sum.

### 3. Fast kill

- **7A:** Develop the exact contribution of one fixed zero and compare it with the interval main term; do not treat that bound as control of the full zero sum.
- **7B:** Killed algebraically: every nonzero finite stencil annihilating constants has coefficients of both signs, so positivity of \(\psi\)'s increments gives no sign for the filtered statistic.
- **7C:** Killed by positivity: \(\widehat w(1)=\int w(u)du>0\) for every nonzero (w\ge0); making it zero requires a signed weight and loses the proposed certificate.

### 4. Develop strongest initial survivor: 7A

For a single zero \(\rho=\beta+i\gamma\), where (1/2<\beta<1), the residue contribution to an interval explicit formula is

\[
Z_\rho(X,h)=\frac{(X+h)^\rho-X^\rho}{\rho}=\int_X^{X+h}t^{\rho-1}\,dt.
\]

Because \(|t^{\rho-1}|=t^{\beta-1}\) and \(\beta-1<0\),

\[
|Z_\rho(X,h)|\le hX^{\beta-1},\qquad \frac{|Z_\rho(X,h)|}{h}\le X^{\beta-1}\longrightarrow0.
\]
The conjugate pair contributes at most (2hX^{\beta-1}). Thus for every fixed zero with β<1, its contribution is (o(h)) relative to the pole/main-term scale (h) of an interval of length (h), including (h=\sqrt X). This proves that positivity of broad prime-power interval counts cannot contradict the contribution of one fixed off-line zero by itself. It says nothing about the collective sum of zeros, the error term after summing them, or a specially designed test that isolates a cluster.

**Explicit 75-digit diagnostic.** For (\beta=3/4), (\gamma=14.1347), (h=\sqrt X), compute the exact complex residue magnitude ratio \(|Z_\rho(X,\sqrt X)|/\sqrt X\). The calculus bound is (X^{-1/4}):

```text
X          actual |Z_rho|/h                                      upper bound X^(-1/4)
1e4        0.099793220271197948065990544278232731270889130310466 0.1
1e8        0.0099998741728444366995704099926669592406500005832939 0.01
1e12       0.00099999987499172751999877598357255669579166997283846 0.001
1e16       0.000099999999874999917275107562751416526264565814774997 0.0001
```

The ratio approaches its rigorous upper bound in this fixed-height example and tends to zero. This numerical check illustrates, but is not used to prove, the exact integral estimate.

### 5. Source audit

The bound is the fundamental theorem of calculus applied to the standard explicit-formula zero term; it uses only (1/2<\beta<1). It does not assert that the complete zero sum is (o(h)), and it does not bound other zeros or truncation errors. The no-go is limited to the proposed strategy of forcing a contradiction from the sign of a broad interval count and one isolated fixed zero. For 7B the sign failure follows from the alternating coefficients; for 7C it follows from \(\widehat w(1)>0\). The archive's established linear-positivity lesson already warns that nonnegative Mellin weights retain the pole-scale mass.

### 6. Discrimination test

| Toy world | Result | Exact step |
|---|---|---|
| (a) Curves over finite fields | **No contradiction from positive counts** | Point counts have a dominant (q^m) term; any fixed eigenvalue with magnitude (q^\beta<q) contributes a lower-order (O(q^{\beta m})) term. Positivity alone does not isolate the Weil bound. |
| (b) Ihara zeta of Ramanujan graphs | **Fails on non-Ramanujan graphs** | A regular graph can have a nontrivial adjacency eigenvalue outside the Ramanujan interval but below the trivial degree eigenvalue; closed-walk counts remain nonnegative because the dominant degree term is larger. |
| (c) Random Euler products with independent uniform phases | **Phase-blind after positive projection** | Taking absolute values or a nonnegative smoothing removes the independent phases; the interval-size estimate then does not recover their zero correlations. |
| (d) Davenport–Heilbronn function | **Prime-count mechanism unavailable** | The prescribed function has no nonnegative von-Mangoldt prime count to which the interval-increment argument applies; replacing it by absolute coefficients discards the zero-sensitive phase. |
| (e) Beurling systems with regular integer counts and bad prime counts | **Fails discrimination** | Their generalized von Mangoldt counting function is nondecreasing, and the same estimate (hX^{\beta-1}=o(h)) holds for each fixed zero with β<1; positivity of the count does not exclude the known off-line zeros. |

### 7. Verdict

**DEAD.** A single off-line zero with real part below 1 is lower order than the main term in a broad positive interval, while the obvious pole-canceling operations introduce signed coefficients and forfeit positivity. This blocks only the isolated-residue/positive-count route; it is not a bound on the collective zero sum and is not a no-go for nonlinear or coupled tests.

### 8. New-idea check

**Not new as an asymptotic estimate.** The identity (Z_\rho(X,h)=\int_X^{X+h}t^{\rho-1}dt) and its (hX^{\beta-1}) bound are direct calculus consequences of the usual explicit formula. The Mellin-transform positivity obstruction is already an established input in the objective; finite differences are standard signed cancellation operators. This pass adds a precise scope statement and a high-precision scale diagnostic, not a new theorem about zero locations.

### 9. Lesson

A positive statistic whose main pole remains present cannot expose one fixed zero with β<1 through sign alone; any viable contradiction must couple several zero contributions or remove the pole through a transformation that preserves an independently proved sign.

### 10. Seed

Can an integer-valued jump invariant combine several explicit-formula frequencies so that their collective oscillation is forced to cross a discrete threshold while its underlying statistic remains nonnegative in the Beurling false world? Next operator: **INVARIANT**.

## Pass 8 — Liouville parity on the rough integers

### 1. Operator used

**INVARIANT.** Attach the integer-valued parity λ(n)=(-1)^Ω(n) to the least-prime-factor filtration and ask whether a rough-number layer has a sign or count invariant that singles out primes.

### 2. Generate three candidates

**8A — LCM jump covolume.** Define J(N)=log lcm(1,…,N), whose jumps occur at prime powers. Claim: its exact jump structure gives an independently evaluable positive invariant that can constrain the prime product.

**8B — N^(1/3) rough Liouville bias.** Put y=N^(1/3), A_N={2≤n≤N : P⁻(n)>y}, and B_N={2≤n≤N : P⁻(n)≤y}, where P⁻(n) is the least prime factor. Claim: the normalized signed bias Σ_{n∈A_N}λ(n)/√|A_N| has a stable prime-selective sign or scale that can be independently controlled.

**8C — Square-root roughness selector.** Define R_N={2≤n≤N : P⁻(n)>√N}. Claim: its positive count is a new lattice-side prime statistic with an independent asymptotic evaluation.

### 3. Fast kill

- **8A:** Killed before development: log lcm(1,…,N)=Σ_{p^k≤N}log p=ψ(N) exactly, the same Chebyshev sum it was meant to evaluate; the scale-separation ledger records this as Pass 31.
- **8B:** Develop the strongest candidate. It is a concrete signed invariant and the cutoff makes its factorization classes exact.
- **8C:** Killed immediately: if n≤N is composite, its least prime factor is at most √n≤√N. Hence R_N consists exactly of primes p>√N, and |R_N|=π(N)−π(√N), the prime count in different notation.

### 4. Develop strongest initial survivor: 8B

For n∈A_N, every prime factor, counted with multiplicity, exceeds N^(1/3). If Ω(n)≥3, then n>N, impossible. Thus each n∈A_N is either a prime or a semiprime; squares p² are included among the latter. Therefore λ(n)=−1 on primes and +1 on semiprimes in A_N.

Let a_N=|A_N|, b_N=Σ_{n∈A_N}λ(n), π_A=# {p≤N : p>N^(1/3)}, and s_A=# {n∈A_N : n composite}. Then the exact identities are
- a_N=π_A+s_A;
- b_N=−π_A+s_A;
- π(N)−π(N^(1/3))=(a_N−b_N)/2.

The last identity is exact but not an independent prime estimate: the unsieved signed bias b_N is precisely what must be controlled to recover the prime count. The complementary set count a_N can be computed by finite inclusion-exclusion, but that does not evaluate b_N.
