**Reproducible finite computation.** A smallest-prime-factor sieve computes λ(n)=−λ(n/P⁻(n)), then partitions 2≤n≤N by P⁻(n)>N^(1/3). Normalized biases below were evaluated from the exact integer numerator and square-root denominator at 60 decimal digits:

| N | y=N^(1/3) | A_N count | Σ_A λ(n) | normalized rough bias | B_N count | Σ_B λ(n) | normalized B_N bias |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 100 | 4.64158883361277889 | 32 | -14 | -2.47487373415291633540295526736697163749692578190965912805919 | 67 | 11 | 1.34386388791935745775359314883691379175649783538027521781692 |
| 500 | 7.93700525984099737 | 114 | -68 | -6.36878351875551896785248797947814318058943480985367936623255 | 385 | 47 | 2.39534179975684005979408357945855426840702135749456018060363 |
| 1000 | 10 | 227 | -101 | -6.70360544715971685093042894026431789081908388931085540531854 | 772 | 86 | 3.09520772821938649669740685370066238580646587865332874135485 |
| 5000 | 17.0997594667669699 | 900 | -424 | -14.1333333333333333333333333333333333333333333333333333333333 | 4099 | 377 | 5.88846897296505169874834790963143998397293393577957255653644 |
| 10000 | 21.5443469003188372 | 1710 | -732 | -17.7016205026842847967433824363996779170334310960492440046930 | 8289 | 637 | 6.99662121375271160722922329579652067642834354834309469047015 |

Exact channel checks give:

| N | rough primes π_A | rough semiprimes s_A | −π_A+s_A=b_N | (a_N−b_N)/2 |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 100 | 23 | 9 | -14 | 23 |
| 500 | 91 | 23 | -68 | 91 |
| 1000 | 164 | 63 | -101 | 164 |
| 5000 | 662 | 238 | -424 | 662 |
| 10000 | 1221 | 489 | -732 | 1221 |

Across these five cutoffs, the magnitude of the normalized rough bias increases (2.47, 6.37, 6.70, 14.13, 17.70); this finite sample does not determine an asymptotic rate. The exact decomposition explains the finite values: primes contribute −π_A, and rough semiprimes contribute +s_A. The measurements give no asymptotic estimate of their difference or uniform limiting law.

For Re(s)>1, the ordinary Liouville Dirichlet series is
Σ_{n≥1} λ(n)n^(−s) = ∏_p(1+p^(−s))^(−1) = ζ(2s)/ζ(s).
This is an exact bridge to the zeta function, but it is not a positivity statement. For a generalized-prime system with Euler product ζ_B(s)=∏_q(1−q^(−s))^(−1), the analogous parity series is ζ_B(2s)/ζ_B(s) in its absolute-convergence half-plane. If ρ is a zero of ζ_B with Re(ρ)>1/2, then Re(2ρ)>1, where the convergent Euler product makes ζ_B(2ρ) nonzero; absent numerator cancellation, ζ_B(2s)/ζ_B(s) has a pole at ρ with the denominator's zero multiplicity. Thus the ratio records such a zero rather than excluding it.

### 5. Source audit

The signs come from the definition (-1)^Ω(n), not from a positive quadratic form or an independently evaluated prime observable. The rough-class decomposition is elementary. The Dirichlet identity follows from (1+p^(−s))^(−1)=(1−p^(−2s))/(1−p^(−s)) and absolute convergence for Re(s)>1. The inability of ordinary sieve data to distinguish parity of the number of prime factors is the classical sieve parity phenomenon; see Heath-Brown, “A parity problem from sieve theory,” Mathematika 29 (1982), https://doi.org/10.1112/S0025579300012109, and Tao's notes on the sieve parity problem, https://terrytao.wordpress.com/wp-content/uploads/2008/04/whatsnew_onlineversion1.pdf. Those sources identify the known obstruction class; they do not prove a no-go for every possible estimate of this particular signed sum.

### 6. Discrimination test

| Toy world | Result | Exact step |
|---|---|---|
| (a) Curves over finite fields | **No transfer of the required conclusion** | Closed-point degrees give an Euler product and cohomological Frobenius gives its zeros, but there is no canonical least-prime-factor filtration on degrees; a parity series alone does not derive the Weil bound. |
| (b) Ihara zeta of Ramanujan graphs | **No spectral-bound transfer** | Primitive cycles give graph Euler factors, but an integer Ω/least-prime-factor split is not defined canonically on cycles; a cycle-parity Euler ratio would not prove the Ramanujan spectral bound. |
| (c) Random Euler products with independent uniform phases | **Fails phase discrimination** | The statistic uses only exponent parity and positive integer magnitudes; independent local phases are absent. Inserting phases as extra data changes the object and gives no sign constraint on their zeros. |
| (d) Davenport–Heilbronn function | **Unavailable from its data** | There is no canonical Euler factorization from which to define the multiplicative parity coefficients or rough-number filtration. |
| (e) Beurling systems with regular integer counts and bad prime counts | **Fails discrimination** | The generalized parity series has the same quotient form and records an off-line zero as a pole; it remains available in the false world and supplies no contradiction or positivity ruling that zero out. |

The candidate fails the mandatory true-world transfer and false-world discrimination tests. It can encode zero data algebraically, but it supplies no selective sign or estimate separating the stipulated true and false systems.

### 7. Verdict

**DEAD.** The exact identity π(N)−π(N^(1/3))=(|A_N|−Σ_{A_N}λ)/2 reduces the rough prime count to a signed parity sum, while finite computations give only finite values. Its Euler ratio records off-line zeros in false generalized-prime systems, and positivity of sieve counts does not control the signed parity bias. The rough decomposition is elementary, not a new result or a bound for the target functional.

### 8. New-idea check

The Dirichlet-series identity for Liouville and the sieve parity barrier are standard. The N^(1/3) rough-set split is an elementary specialization of the fact that three factors each exceeding N^(1/3) have product greater than N. No literature novelty is claimed. The finite sieve table is a project diagnostic only. The lcm-jump candidate repeats scale_separation_search.md Pass 31, and the square-root selector is the elementary least-prime-factor characterization of primes.

### 9. Lesson

A parity-weighted rough layer can isolate a prime count only by leaving the signed prime-versus-semiprime bias as the unknown; require the next invariant to evaluate that bias through independent data and fail on a bad generalized-prime model.

### 10. Seed

Can an arithmetic invariant couple (-1)^Ω(n) to a second independently evaluated local statistic so that its prime/semiprime cancellation is controlled without using the generalized system's zero locations? Next operator: **RISING-SEA**.

## Pass 9 — Symmetric prime-log Herglotz transform

### 1. Operator used

**RISING-SEA.** Define the missing structure axiomatically, derive its consequences, then test whether known arithmetic objects satisfy the axioms without inserting zero locations.

### 2. Generate three candidates

**9A — Symmetric prime-log Herglotz measure.** For a cutoff X and real σ>1, put a positive atom of weight (log p) p^(−kσ)/2 at each of ±k log p for every prime power p^k≤X, and take its Cauchy transform. Test positivity, reflection, Euler-data recovery, and zero selectivity.

**9B — Pick condition on the regularized logarithmic derivative.** Set G(s)=−ζ′(s)/ζ(s)−1/(s−1) and demand that G be a positive-real/Pick function on Re(s)>1/2. This is killed at screening: holomorphy there already excludes the poles arising from zeros in that half-plane, so the condition imports the sought zero-free conclusion as an axiom and is not an independently constructed object.

**9C — Reflected finite Euler product.** Set Z_P(s)=∏_{p≤P}(1−p^(−s))^(−1) and R_P(s)=Z_P(s)Z_P(1−s). This is killed at screening as a repeat of the imposed-reflection candidates in Passes 1C and 3B: reflection is automatic, while finite local factors do not give the completed Xi identity.

### 3. Develop 9A

For X≥2 and σ>1 define the finite positive measure

ν_{X,σ} = (1/2) Σ_{p^k≤X} (log p) p^(−kσ) [δ_{k log p}+δ_{−k log p}].

For Im(z)>0 define its Cauchy transform

F_{X,σ}(z)=∫(u−z)^(−1) dν_{X,σ}(u).

The measure is positive and symmetric. Directly,

Im F_{X,σ}(z) = (Im z) ∫ |u−z|^(−2) dν_{X,σ}(u) > 0,

because ν is nonzero. Thus F is a Herglotz/Pick function. Symmetry gives F_{X,σ}(−z)=−F_{X,σ}(z). At z=iy with y>0,

F_{X,σ}(iy)=iy Σ_{p^k≤X} (log p) p^(−kσ) / ((k log p)^2+y^2),

so its imaginary part is strictly positive. The total mass is

ν_{X,σ}(R)=Σ_{p^k≤X}(log p)p^(−kσ).

As X→∞ this tends, for real σ>1, to −ζ′(σ)/ζ(σ) by the absolutely convergent Euler-product logarithmic derivative. This is an exact multiplicative-to-additive encoding: prime powers become atoms at their logarithms. The reflection of the measure is imposed by adding the paired atoms; it is not derived from the completed zeta functional equation.

For a reproducible finite check, take X=7 and σ=2. The included powers are 2, 3, 4, 5, and 7. High-precision evaluation gives

- total mass: 0.4427664945181183405585825117386786515727829252956254051115189485523543;
- F_{7,2}(i)=0.2134152577664423097767131917750941956267440915685833696063099902344189 i;
- F_{7,2}(2i)=0.1686074998357750866376457653758104295356991112967988438240243219984483 i.

These computations verify the finite formulas; they are not evidence for a bound on zeta zeros.

### 4. Five-property test

- **P1 (multiplicative/additive bridge): holds at the coefficient level.** The Euler logarithmic derivative has coefficients (log p)p^(−kσ), and the logarithm map places each prime power at k log p exactly. The construction does not use zero locations. It does not yet give a Fourier transform or completed-Xi identity.
- **P2 (intrinsic positivity): holds for this object.** The measure is positive, and the displayed Herglotz identity proves positive imaginary part in the upper half-plane. Positivity follows from the positive Euler weights, not from a fitted sign.
- **P3 (functional-equation symmetry): fails as an arithmetic consequence.** F(−z)=−F(z) follows only from the artificially reflected support. No map has been proved from z↦−z to s↦1−s for the completed zeta function, and the pole and gamma factors are absent.
- **P4 (canonical Xi identification): fails.** F is a Cauchy transform with singularities at the real support points ±k log p after continuation. No determinant or spectral identity identifies those singularities or any determinant of F with zeros of Xi.
- **P5 (zero visibility): fails.** The construction uses prime powers only; its support is the set of logarithmic prime powers, not the zero ordinates. No theorem maps its spectral data to Xi zeros.

### 5. Source audit

The Herglotz property is proved directly from Im(1/(u−z))=(Im z)/|u−z|² for Im(z)>0. The mass identity is the standard absolutely convergent identity −ζ′(σ)/ζ(σ)=Σ_{n≥2}Λ(n)n^(−σ), with the finite measure simply truncating that series at n≤X. The symmetric pairing is a design choice. None of these steps uses a new theorem about zero locations or yields an Xi determinant.

### 6. Discrimination test

| Toy world | Result | Exact step |
|---|---|---|
| (a) Curves over finite fields | **No Weil-bound transfer** | A positive measure on logarithmic closed-point weights has a Herglotz transform by the same integral identity; that identity alone places no bound on Frobenius eigenvalues. The curve's cohomological determinant requires additional structure absent here. |
| (b) Ihara zeta of graphs | **No Ramanujan transfer** | Positive cycle weights again give the same Pick positivity. A non-Ramanujan graph still has positive cycle-count weights, so positivity of this transform does not force the nontrivial adjacency spectrum into the Ramanujan interval. |
| (c) Independent-phase Euler products | **Phase information erased** | The weights use only positive magnitudes (log p)p^(−kσ). Changing independent local phases leaves this positive measure unchanged, so the transform cannot discriminate phase-sensitive zero geometry. |
| (d) Davenport–Heilbronn function | **No canonical instantiation** | Without a canonical Euler product there is no prescribed prime-power measure of this form. Choosing artificial atoms would add data rather than derive it from the function. |
| (e) Generalized-prime false worlds | **Fails discrimination** | Replace p by generalized primes q and use positive weights (log q)q^(−kσ). The same positivity and reflection proof works regardless of the associated zeta's zero locations; the construction therefore survives in the stipulated bad-zero world. |

The Herglotz theorem is universal for positive measures, so its positivity is intentionally too generic to separate the desired system from false worlds.

### 7. Verdict

**DEAD.** The candidate gives a clean, exact positive transform of prime-power logarithmic data, but its positivity and odd reflection persist under arbitrary generalized-prime substitution. Its support singularities are logarithmic prime powers, and no canonical determinant or zero map connects it to Xi. The useful result is limited to an explicit Herglotz encoding of the Euler logarithmic derivative's positive coefficients in its convergence region.

### 8. New-idea check

Not new: the positive-measure Cauchy transform is the standard Herglotz/Pick construction, and −ζ′/ζ has its standard Euler-series expansion for σ>1. The candidate combines them in a symmetric log-prime measure, but the reflection was inserted by hand and no new zero-sensitive statement follows. The 9B screening repeats the general circularity of assuming the needed half-plane regularity; 9C repeats the reflected finite-Euler-product obstruction already recorded in Passes 1C and 3B.

### 9. Lesson

A positive Euler-weight measure automatically yields a Pick transform in both true and false generalized-prime systems; a viable candidate must derive a zero-selective constraint from an additional arithmetic or geometric structure rather than from positive weights and imposed reflection alone.

### 10. Seed

Can a canonical local-to-global pairing couple the positive prime-log measure to the archimedean and pole terms so that the combined transform changes under bad zero geometry while retaining a directly proved positivity? Next operator: **TOY-FIRST**.
## Pass 10 — Consolidation: positivity that survives false worlds

### 1. Verdict and operator audit

Passes 1–9 each used one distinct operator in the prescribed rotation. All nine developed candidates were marked DEAD; no pass-level PARTIAL or CANDIDATE verdict was recorded. The ledger does contain two proved subsidiary statements: Pass 1A's standard finite positive-weight Gram identity, and Pass 5A's elementary impossibility of realizing q/(1−q) as the logarithmic derivative of a finite scalar polynomial. Neither is a partial proof of the target. The scorecard is therefore consistent: each operator has one pass, zero partial verdicts, and three killed ideas.

### 2. Most frequent death step

The dominant recurring failure is false-world nondiscrimination. Each developed candidate, 1A through 9A/B as selected in the pass narratives (specifically 1A, 2A, 3A, 4A, 5A, 6A, 7A, 8B, and 9A), retains its claimed positivity, symmetry, or order-theoretic property after replacement by a generalized-prime system with bad zero locations. This is a count of the nine developed candidates, not of every fast-killed variant; several other variants died earlier for their own exact algebraic defects.

The selection-bias audit finds a clear pattern: the generated candidates were disproportionately built by importing positivity first (positive measures, sums of squares, Hodge forms, positive Hamiltonians, or monotone counts) and asking afterward whether that positivity knew about zeros. The mandatory false-world test then rejected them. The search has not yet adequately sampled constructions where the sign is produced by a coupling among prime, pole, and archimedean terms, or where a negative test would force an independently checkable finite arithmetic witness. The repeated failures do not prove that every possible construction is false-world invariant.

### 3. Scoped no-go result for measure-only positivity certificates

**Proposition.** Let ν be any finite positive measure on a real space Ω. For test functions φ_1,…,φ_m, the matrix G with entries G_{ij}=∫_Ω φ_i(x) conjugate(φ_j(x)) dν(x) is positive semidefinite, since for every c∈C^m,

c*Gc = ∫_Ω |Σ_i conjugate(c_i)φ_i(x)|² dν(x) ≥ 0.

Likewise, for a positive measure ν on R and z in the upper half-plane, its Cauchy transform F_ν(z)=∫(u−z)^(−1)dν(u) satisfies Im F_ν(z)=(Im z)∫|u−z|^(−2)dν(u)≥0. Consequently, if the false generalized-prime world supplies a positive measure and the proposed certificate derives its sign solely from one of these identities (or another measure-positivity identity with the same hypotheses), that sign holds in the false world too. Such a certificate alone cannot distinguish the target property from that false world.

**Proof.** Expand the quadratic form under the integral to obtain the displayed integral of an absolute square. For the Cauchy transform, take the imaginary part of the integrand pointwise. Both proofs use only ν≥0; no arithmetic or zero-location property enters. Substitution of any other positive measure therefore preserves the conclusions. ∎

**Scope.** This proves a no-go for the stated measure-only certificate class and explains the repeated Pass 1A/9A failure. It does not cover pairings that use signed arithmetic data, a canonical completion, cutoff-uniform estimates, or additional geometric identities whose hypotheses fail in the false world. The proposition is an elementary abstraction of standard Gram/Herglotz positivity, not a new result about the target sign.

### 4. Three most promising unexplored seeds

1. Couple the prime-log measure to the pole and archimedean distributions by one independently defined pairing; test the full object in the bad generalized-prime world before pursuing positivity.
2. Assume a negative test for the complete Weil form and seek a finite arithmetic witness with a quantitative stability bound under support growth; do not infer it from positivity of a selected component.
3. For the localized spectral family, seek an operator-specific omitted-tail estimate smaller than the actual parity gap; finite-section monotonicity or adjacent-cutoff drift alone is insufficient.

### 5. Operator schedule for Passes 11–20

| pass | operator | required emphasis |
|---:|---|---|
| 11 | TOY-FIRST | Re-derive the finite-field positivity mechanism from its geometric pairing, then identify which exact ingredient has no integer-side analogue; avoid using Frobenius eigenvalues as input data. |
| 12 | SPECIALIZE | Analyze the complete Weil form at the first prime atom, retaining pole and gamma terms, and compute an exact finite witness or obstruction rather than a prime-only truncation. |
| 13 | GENERALIZE | Build a cutoff/deformation family for the full form and prove a uniform relation between its error and the true support-dependent positivity margin. |
| 14 | CHANGE-THE-OBJECT | Convert a negative complete-form test, if one exists, into an integer or prime-power certificate and prove the conversion is stable under limiting support. |
| 15 | IMPORT | Test whether an arithmetic intersection/Hodge pairing can couple finite and archimedean places canonically, with the bad-world test applied to the pairing itself. |
| 16 | COUNTEREXAMPLE-ANATOMY | Assume a negative test exists and minimize support or a norm; derive exact Euler/pole/gamma consequences and check whether compactness yields a finite witness. |
| 17 | IMPOSSIBILITY | Seek a discrete invariant forced by the complete explicit formula under a hypothetical off-line zero; a mere larger error term does not count. |
| 18 | INVARIANT | Construct an integer-valued or monotone joint prime/pole statistic, with its complement explicitly controlled and its value defined in false generalized-prime models. |
| 19 | RISING-SEA | Axiomatically specify a local-to-global pairing whose positivity is not a consequence of a positive measure alone; test existence against the false worlds before deriving target consequences. |
| 20 | CONSOLIDATION | Audit all ten new passes, update the frequency counts and seeds, and attempt the next scoped no-go theorem. |

The existing nine-operator rotation restarts at Pass 11. Pass 20 is reserved for the mandatory tenth-pass consolidation. Each pass must still generate three computable candidates, fast-kill two where warranted, and fully test the strongest survivor against all five worlds.

### 6. Consolidation verdict

The measure-only positivity class has a proved, limited false-world no-go; no candidate in Passes 1–9 discriminates all five worlds, and no target-sign statement has been established. The next cycle is constrained to build the arithmetic/pole/archimedean coupling or a stable finite witness before claiming that positivity is selective.
## Pass 11 — Elliptic-curve degree positivity and prime translations

### 1. Operator used

**TOY-FIRST.** Re-derive the simplest geometric proof of a true spectral bound, isolate the exact source of its sign and trace, and test that mechanism on a finite prime-translation algebra.

### 2. Generate three candidates

**11A — Prime-translation Rosati moment state.** Let Γ=(Z/5Z)^2 with generators e₂,e₃ and shifts U_g on ℓ²(Γ). Seek a normalized positive state τ on the group algebra whose prime moments reproduce the normalized Weil coefficients τ(U_{e_p})=−log(p)/√p, while mixed-prime moments remain zero as they do in the prime-only distribution. Claim: a degree-like quadratic form polarized from τ is PSD and carries a nonzero trace cross term analogous to the Frobenius degree form. Develop this candidate against the first two primes.

**11B — Local prime-label Frobenius forms.** For each prime p, define H_p(x,y)=x²−(log p)xy+py² and tensor the local quadratic forms over p≤X. Claim: the local discriminants yield a positive arithmetic degree whose product controls the global prime Euler data. This is killed immediately as a selector: log²p<4p makes each form positive for every p, and replacing p by any generalized-prime size preserves the same elementary positivity without controlling zeros.

**11C — Chosen elliptic Frobenius factors.** For each p choose an elliptic curve E_p/F_p, set a_p=p+1−#E_p(F_p), and form L_X(s)=∏_{p≤X}(1−a_pp^(−s)+p^(1−2s))^(−1). Claim: the product of the local positive degree forms supplies a canonical Xi-like determinant. This is killed at screening: the E_p are arbitrary extra inputs, Hasse's local bound gives no global zero statement for this product, and changing the curves changes the function while leaving each local degree argument intact.

### 3. Fast kill

- **11A:** Develop the degree analogy and test the exact normalized positive-state requirement on the four group elements 0,e₂,e₃,e₂+e₃.
- **11B:** The matrix is positive because its discriminant is negative for every prime, but the same inequality holds for arbitrary q>1 and contains no zero-sensitive data; the tensor product adds no coupling.
- **11C:** Local curves and their Frobenius traces are freely supplied rather than derived from the integer prime structure; the local Hasse theorem does not identify the product with Xi or force its zeros onto a line.

### 4. Develop strongest initial survivor: 11A

#### The true toy calculation

Take E/F₅ given by y²=x³−x. For x=0,1,2,3,4, the numbers of y-values are respectively 1,1,2,2,1; adding the point at infinity gives #E(F₅)=8. Thus its Frobenius trace is a=5+1−8=−2, and the characteristic polynomial is

T²−aT+5=T²+2T+5.

Its roots are −1±2i, with exact squared modulus 1+4=5. The geometric proof is not merely that a norm is positive. On End(E), degree is a positive quadratic form, deg(F)=5, and the trace is recovered by polarization: deg(1−F)=#E(F₅)=8, so 1+deg(F)−deg(1−F)=−2. More generally, for integers m,n,

deg(nF−[m])=5n²+2mn+m²≥0.

After division by n² and density of rational m/n in R, the polynomial t²+2t+5 is nonnegative for all real t. Its discriminant is 2²−4·5=−16, which forces the Frobenius roots to be a conjugate pair; their product 5 then gives modulus √5. This is the exact mechanism transferred from the elliptic-curve proof. Silverman, *The Arithmetic of Elliptic Curves*, Chapter V, Theorem 2.3.1, gives this degree-map proof and the resulting zeta numerator [source](https://www.math.ens.psl.eu/~obenoist/refs/Silverman.pdf).

#### Prime-translation attempt

Let Γ=(Z/5Z)^2 and let U₂,U₃ be the regular shifts by e₂,e₃ on the 25-dimensional space ℓ²(Γ). The Weil prime coefficients at the first two primes have normalized magnitudes

a₂=log(2)/√2=0.4901290717342735958569508618176166906457303495495273605211231235326456,

a₃=log(3)/√3=0.6342841005975639771863877725446490551995367019292008127561603787185373.

The concrete positive-state claim asks for a normalized positive-definite coefficient function τ with τ(0)=1, τ(±e₂)=−a₂, τ(±e₃)=−a₃, and τ(±e₂±e₃)=0. These are the prime-only moments with no mixed-prime channel. Its required 4×4 Gram matrix on 0,e₂,e₃,e₂+e₃ is

M = [[1, −a₂, −a₃, 0], [−a₂, 1, 0, −a₃], [−a₃, 0, 1, −a₂], [0, −a₃, −a₂, 1]].

Its four eigenvalues are 1±a₂±a₃. In particular,

λ_min(M)=1−a₂−a₃=−0.1244131723318375730433386343622657458452670514787281732772835022511828,

and the vector (1,−1,−1,1) has quadratic value

(1,−1,−1,1) M (1,−1,−1,1)^T = −0.4976526893273502921733545374490629833810682059149126931091340090047313.

The strict negativity has an exact elementary certificate, not just a decimal approximation. The logarithm series gives log 2>2/3 and log 3>1. Also √2>7/5 and √3<7/4. Hence a₂>√2/3>7/15 and a₃>1/√3>4/7, so a₂+a₃>109/105>1. Thus no unit-mass positive state can have these two prime moments and zero mixed moments.

The canonical regular trace on the finite group algebra makes the other side of the dilemma explicit. For A=a₂U₂+a₃U₃, Tr(A)=0 because every nonidentity translation has zero trace. Moreover Tr(A* A)=25(a₂²+a₃²), so

Tr((nA−mI)*(nA−mI))=25[n²(a₂²+a₃²)+m²]≥0.

Here a₂²+a₃²=(log 2)²/2+(log 3)²/3=0.6425428272299613716148109711131211237380975913403467883723410135, and this expression has no mn cross term at all. A check including the prime square 4 uses A=a₂U₂+(log 2)/2·U₂²+a₃U₃; its exact normalized squared energy per group element is 0.7626560807095117277815866026947873666707358292389831850890570469038209, and its trace remains zero. The finite positive norm is real but supplies none of the Frobenius trace coefficient that drives the Hasse discriminant argument.

#### Exact claim and obstruction

The proposed transfer claim is that one canonical prime-translation degree form both (i) has the Frobenius-style polarized cross term equal to the signed von-Mangoldt sample and (ii) is positive. With the canonical regular trace, (i) fails because nonidentity shifts have trace zero. If the trace is changed to force the first two prime samples while retaining unit identity mass and zero mixed-prime moments, (ii) fails by the negative four-point Gram minor. This is an exact obstruction to this normalized prime-only moment model; it does not rule out a full completion with nonzero mixed moments supplied by the pole and archimedean terms.

### 5. Source audit

In the finite-field toy, positivity comes from the geometric degree map on elliptic-curve endomorphisms, while the trace comes from the same degree polarization. The proof's leverage is the identity deg(1−F)=#E(F_q), not an arbitrary Hilbert norm. In the prime-translation model, positivity of the regular Hilbert-space norm is independent of prime data and has zero trace on nonidentity shifts. The custom moment alternative is contradicted by the exact 4×4 principal Gram matrix. No source assumption equivalent to the target is used; no conclusion about the completed Weil form follows.

### 6. Discrimination test

| Toy world | Result | Exact step |
|---|---|---|
| (a) Curves over finite fields | **True toy succeeds; integer transfer fails** | For E/F₅ the degree form gives a=−2 and the exact discriminant −16. The prime-translation state cannot retain the first two normalized prime moments with unit mass and zero mixed moments. |
| (b) Ihara zeta of graphs | **No Ramanujan-bound transfer** | A Hilbert–Schmidt degree Tr((nA−mI)*(nA−mI)) is nonnegative for every adjacency matrix. A non-Ramanujan regular graph still has this positivity, so no bound on nontrivial adjacency eigenvalues follows. |
| (c) Random Euler products with independent uniform phases | **Phase-blind** | The regular-shift norm uses only coefficient magnitudes and is unchanged when local phases vary; adding phases to coefficients does not change the universal Hilbert–Schmidt positivity, while the unit-mass moment variant is tested only on the ordinary prime magnitudes and has no phase rule. |
| (d) Davenport–Heilbronn function | **No canonical instantiation** | It has no Euler prime translations from which to define A. Replacing them with arbitrary Dirichlet coefficients restores only a generic positive norm, which has no link to its off-line zeros. |
| (e) Beurling systems with regular integer counts and bad prime counts | **Fails discrimination** | Replacing p by generalized primes q gives the same regular-shift Hilbert–Schmidt identity and nonnegative degree. Its proof uses only unitarity of shifts and holds even when the generalized zeta has off-line zeros. The custom ordinary-prime moment state already fails before this world is reached. |

### 7. Verdict

**DEAD.** The true elliptic-curve proof depends on a geometric degree whose polarization recovers Frobenius trace. The canonical prime-translation trace erases every nonidentity prime shift, while forcing the first two prime coefficients into a normalized positive state creates an exact negative 4×4 minor. The generic Hilbert–Schmidt replacement is positive in false worlds and has no target-selective cross term. This is candidate-specific, not a no-go for a full prime–pole–archimedean pairing.

### 8. New-idea check

The finite-field degree proof is classical, as documented in Silverman, Chapter V, Theorem 2.3.1. The prime-translation Hilbert–Schmidt identity is standard finite-group representation theory, and the four-point test is the ordinary positive-definiteness condition for a moment matrix. The exact {2,3} incompatibility is a new diagnostic for this particular unit-mass, prime-only assignment, not a new theorem about zeta zeros or a universal obstruction.

### 9. Lesson

A positive degree norm transfers a spectral bound only when its polarization independently produces the correct arithmetic trace; test the mixed-prime moment matrix before importing geometric positivity, and require the pole/archimedean completion to explain any missing mixed moments.

### 10. Seed

Can the complete Weil form's pole and gamma contributions supply exactly the mixed-prime moments missing from the failed 4×4 Gram matrix at shifts log 2, log 3, and log 6, with an independently proved positive completion? Next operator: **SPECIALIZE**.
## Pass 12 — First prime atom with the complete Weil form

### 1. Operator used

**SPECIALIZE.** Restrict to the first prime atom while keeping the pole, constant, and archimedean terms in the fixed Bombieri normalization. The finite support is chosen so the autocorrelation sees the (p=2) atom but no other prime power.

### 2. Generate three candidates

**12A — First-atom remainder audit.** Let (h_a(x)=\exp(-1/(1-(x/a)^2)^2)) for (|x|<a), and zero outside, with (a=0.49). Its autocorrelation support radius is (2a=0.98), so (log 2<2a<log 3). Claim: the complete non-prime remainder (Q(h_a)-Q_{p=2}(h_a)) can be evaluated explicitly and compared with the negative first-prime term.

**12B — Radius-difference pole cancellation.** Compare two bump tests with different radii and choose a scalar coefficient to cancel their pole masses, hoping the remaining difference isolates the (p=2) term with a stable sign. Claim: a two-radius contrast gives a prime-selective inequality without discarding the gamma contribution.

**12C — Prime-only local Gram.** Compress translates of the same bump to the span generated by the identity and the first shift (log 2), and use the (2\times2) prime-term Gram as a finite positivity certificate. Claim: positivity of this local Gram controls the complete form immediately after the first atom enters.

### 3. Fast kill

- **12A:** Develop: this is a direct finite diagnostic of the complete normalization and does not drop any term.
- **12B:** Killed at the cancellation claim: cancelling one scalar pole value between two different tests does not cancel the distinct archimedean integrals, and choosing the coefficient to cancel the unknown full remainder would be circular.
- **12C:** Killed at the implication: a Gram matrix for the prime term alone omits the pole/gamma balance, so its sign cannot certify the complete quadratic form; this repeats the requirement in the normalization and lessons ledgers to retain every component.

### 4. Develop 12A

Use the fixed test
\[
h(x)=\begin{cases}\exp\!\left(-\dfrac{1}{(1-(x/0.49)^2)^2}\right),&|x|<0.49,\\0,&|x|\ge0.49.\end{cases}
\]
Set (g=h*\widetilde h). Then (g) is even and supported in ([-0.98,0.98]). Since
\[
\log 2=0.69314718055994530941723212145817656807550013436026<0.98<1.0986122886681096913952452369225257046474905578227=\log 3,
\]
the only integer (n\ge2) with (Lambda(n)g(\log n)\ne0) is (n=2). The exact arithmetic decomposition is therefore
\[
Q(h)=R_{\mathrm{pole}+\mathrm{constant}+\Gamma}(h)-2\frac{\log2}{\sqrt2}g(\log2).
\]
The normalization block's 55-digit quadrature check gives
\[
Q(h)=0.000125395798571955035372185087167614677036996996,
\]
\[
Q_{p=2}(h)=-0.00001788717776119892640713173231794513518406.
\]
Subtracting yields the complete non-prime remainder
\[
R_{\mathrm{pole}+\mathrm{constant}+\Gamma}(h)=0.000143282976333153961779316819485559812221056996.
\]
Thus the remainder is about (8.0103735897322526162224606126905256764) times the magnitude of the prime term; the prime term lowers this particular value by about (12.4838122566693556762429603501111419\%.) The underlying prime autocorrelation value inferred from the same exact coefficient is
\[
g(\log2)=0.00001824741562248787262681138645976470244015410733166638917.
\]
This computation retains pole, constant, and gamma contributions together through the exact remainder subtraction. It verifies one test value; it does not establish a lower bound for the remainder on all admissible (h), nor any support-uniform estimate.

### 5. Source audit

The coefficients and support localization follow directly from the stated Bombieri normalization. The numerical (Q)-value and prime contribution are the already-recorded high-precision normalization audit in `quadratic_positivity_search.md`; the new arithmetic is the exact support classification and subtraction of the first-prime term. No zero ordinates enter. The positive remainder is a numerical value for this one test, not a proved positive operator or a general inequality.

### 6. Discrimination test

| Toy world | Result | Exact step |
|---|---|---|
| (a) Curves over finite fields | **No transfer** | A finite-field closed-point weight can be matched locally, but the real-log bump autocorrelation has no map to the Frobenius characteristic polynomial or its cohomological degree pairing. |
| (b) Ihara zeta of graphs | **No Ramanujan transfer** | Matching one shortest-cycle term does not constrain the nontrivial adjacency spectrum; the pole/gamma remainder has no graph-theoretic realization in this test. |
| (c) Random Euler products | **Fails phase sensitivity** | The calculation uses only the magnitude and location of the (p=2) coefficient; changing independent local phases does not alter this scalar test, so it cannot detect phase-driven zeros. |
| (d) Davenport–Heilbronn | **Unavailable canonically** | The test's prime term is prescribed by an Euler product; the no-Euler-product toy supplies no canonical (p=2) coefficient or matching completed remainder. Adding them externally changes the input rather than deriving them. |
| (e) Beurling false world | **Fails discrimination** | A generalized-prime system can agree on its first local atom and on any chosen finite completion data while differing globally and having off-line zeros. This finite scalar check only uses that local data, so its arithmetic calculation is unchanged and yields no contradiction. |

The method therefore fails the required discrimination test: it does not pass from a single local sign calculation to a spectral constraint in any false world.

### 7. Verdict

**DEAD.** The exact result is a finite, high-precision decomposition for one bump: a negative (p=2) contribution of magnitude (1.7887\times10^{-5}) is outweighed by a numerically positive non-prime remainder, leaving (Q(h)>0). The missing step is a theorem that controls the complete remainder uniformly over all tests and cutoffs; this candidate provides no such bound and is not selective on the toy worlds.

### 8. New-idea check

The Weil-form decomposition and bump test are already present in the normalization block of `quadratic_positivity_search.md`. The pass adds a first-prime support classification and an explicit remainder subtraction from that logged computation, but the underlying test function, formula, and high-precision total are not new. This is a new bookkeeping diagnostic, not a new theorem or an independent positivity mechanism.

### 9. Lesson

A complete-form computation just past one atom can quantify the remainder on a test, but a useful mechanism must prove a uniform remainder inequality and retain enough global arithmetic data to reject the false Beurling model.

### 10. Seed

Can the pole-plus-archimedean remainder be represented by a canonical kernel whose restriction to every prime-atom support window has an explicit lower bound exceeding the corresponding negative prime autocorrelation, with the same representation failing for a bad generalized-prime model? Next operator: **GENERALIZE**.

## Pass 13 — Support-scaled cutoff versus positivity margin

### 1. Operator used

**GENERALIZE.** Embed the full normalized Weil form into a cutoff family indexed by test support radius \(a\), then compare the actual omitted-form error with the support-dependent positivity margin.

### 2. Generate three candidates

**13A — Support-scaled prime cutoff.** For even \(h\) supported in \([-a,a]\), let \(Q_{a,Y}\) retain the complete pole, constant, and archimedean terms but truncate the prime-power sum at \(n\le Y(a)=e^{2a}/4\). Claim: its form-operator error is smaller than the positive margin \(\delta(a)=\inf_{\|h\|_2=1,\,\mathrm{supp}(h)\subset[-a,a]}Q(h)\), uniformly as \(a\) grows.

**13B — Positive Mellin scale average.** For a fixed nonnegative compactly supported weight \(\omega(r)\), define \(\mathcal A_\omega(h)=\int Q(D_rh)\omega(r)\,dr/r\), where \(D_rh(x)=r^{-1/2}h(x/r)\). Claim: averaging small-support forms extends positivity to larger support while retaining completed arithmetic information.

**13C — Two-scale pole cancellation.** Form \(Q(D_rh)-cQ(D_sh)\) for \(r\ne s\), choosing a universal scalar \(c\) to cancel the pole contribution and isolate prime-power channels. Claim: the resulting contrast remains positive semidefinite and prime-selective.

### 3. Fast kill

- **13A:** Retain for development: the error is an explicit finite sum, and a two-lobe test can give a lower bound on its operator norm.
- **13B:** Killed: positivity of a scale average does not imply positivity at each scale; a positive average can conceal negative scales and applies unchanged to false generalized-prime systems.
- **13C:** Killed: dilation changes the pole multiplier to \(2\cosh(rx/2)\), not a scalar multiple of the \(s\)-dilated multiplier for \(r\ne s\); no universal scalar cancels it, and a signed contrast loses positivity.

### 4. Develop 13A: exact omitted-atom lower bound

Write \(g=h*\widetilde h\) and define \(Q_{a,Y}\) by the normalization in quadratic_positivity_search.md, replacing only the prime sum with \(2\le n\le Y\). Since \(\mathrm{supp}(g)\subset[-2a,2a]\),
\[
E_{a,Y}(h):=Q(h)-Q_{a,Y}(h)
=-2\sum_{Y<n\le e^{2a}}\frac{\Lambda(n)}{\sqrt n}\,g(\log n).
\]
For real nonnegative \(h\), every autocorrelation value \(g(u)\) is nonnegative, so the omitted summands have one sign.

Choose \(n=2^k\) to be the largest power of \(2\) strictly below \(e^{2a}\). For \(a\ge\log2\), this gives \(e^{2a}/2\le n<e^{2a}\), hence \(Y(a)<n\le e^{2a}\). Put \(u=\log n\). Choose a nonnegative even smooth bump \(\psi_\epsilon\), \(L^2\)-normalized and supported in \([-\epsilon,\epsilon]\), with \(\epsilon\) small enough that the two translates below are disjoint and contained in \([-a,a]\), and that no other prime-power logarithm in the finite support lies within \(2\epsilon\) of \(u\) or 0. Set
\[
h(x)=\frac{\psi_\epsilon(x-u/2)+\psi_\epsilon(x+u/2)}{\sqrt2}.
\]
Then \(\|h\|_2=1\), \(h\) is even and nonnegative, and direct overlap calculation gives \(g(u)=1/2\); all other omitted prime-power samples vanish. Therefore
\[
|E_{a,Y(a)}(h)|=\frac{\Lambda(2^k)}{\sqrt{2^k}}
=\frac{\log2}{\sqrt n}\ge(\log2)e^{-a}.
\]
Taking the supremum over admissible unit tests proves the operator/form error lower bound
\[
\|Q-Q_{a,Y(a)}\|\ge(\log2)e^{-a}.
\]
The ledger's established input is that the true support margin collapses doubly exponentially. Under that stated margin behavior, this fixed-fraction cutoff error is much larger than \(\delta(a)\) for large \(a\); the proposed uniform error-below-margin estimate fails. This conclusion is about this cutoff schedule, not a negative direction of the full form. If instead \(Y\ge e^{2a}\), the error is identically zero because every prime atom visible to the compactly supported test has been retained; that is the exact finite prime sum already present in \(Q\), not a nontrivial approximation.

**Finite exact/numerical check.** Take \(a=0.8\). Then \(e^{2a}/4=e^{1.6}/4=1.237\ldots\), and the largest dyadic prime power below \(e^{1.6}=4.953\ldots\) is \(n=4\). The bump centers are \(\pm\frac12\log4=\pm\log2=\pm0.693147180559945309417232121458176568\ldots\), which fit inside \([-0.8,0.8]\). With \(\epsilon\) sufficiently small, the only nonzero prime-power autocorrelation among the omitted terms is \(g(\log4)=1/2\). Thus the exact error is
\[
E=-\frac{\log2}{2}=-0.346573590279972654708616060729088284\ldots .
\]
The displayed decimal is the numerical value of an exact expression; no quadrature or zero data are used.

### 5. Source audit

The cutoff error identity follows by subtracting two instances of the archived Bombieri normalization; the lower-bound witness uses only a smooth two-lobe test and the exact coefficient \(\Lambda(2^k)=\log2\). The comparison with the rapidly collapsing margin uses the archive's stated margin behavior and is conditional on that recorded input; this pass does not derive a new formula for \(\delta(a)\). No positivity is assumed to obtain the error lower bound, and no zero locations enter.

### 6. Discrimination test for the developed mechanism

| Toy world | Result | Exact step |
|---|---|---|
| (a) Curves over finite fields | **No transfer** | The real support scale \(a\), log atom positions, and two-lobe test have no canonical map to Frobenius degrees or the curve's intersection pairing. |
| (b) Ihara zeta of Ramanujan graphs | **No transfer** | A finite omitted cycle-length coefficient is not an adjacency eigenvalue bound; the error witness supplies no Ramanujan criterion. |
| (c) Random Euler products | **Fails discrimination** | The error norm uses only atom locations and absolute Euler weights, so independent local phases and the associated zero statistics do not affect the witness. |
| (d) Davenport–Heilbronn | **Unavailable canonically** | Without an Euler product, the \(\Lambda(n)\)-weighted atom sum is not defined from the toy function; supplying it externally changes the input. |
| (e) Beurling generalized primes with bad zero behavior | **Fails discrimination** | The same finite-atom truncation and two-lobe witness apply to a generalized-prime system with a matched atom in the omitted band, regardless of its off-line zeros; the error mechanism therefore does not distinguish the false world. |

### 7. Verdict

**DEAD.** The exact omitted-form error for \(Y=e^{2a}/4\) has norm at least \((\log2)e^{-a}\), so it cannot track the ledger's doubly-exponentially collapsing support margin. This does not rule out a cutoff that retains all visible atoms or a different regularization with a proved inverse bound.

### 8. New-idea check

Cutoff forms and localized smooth tests are standard and already appear separately in the archive. The specific support-tied cutoff \(Y=e^{2a}/4\) and its explicit two-lobe lower-bound witness are a new diagnostic here, but they do not provide a new positivity theorem or zero-selective mechanism.

### 9. Lesson

For a growing-support truncation, measure the actual form-error norm against the true positivity margin; a fixed-fraction prime cutoff leaves an exponentially sized witness and cannot track a doubly-exponentially small margin.

### 10. Seed

Can an analytic scale regularization replace a hard prime cutoff while providing an independently proved inverse-error bound below the support margin and failing in the stipulated bad generalized-prime models?

## Pass 14 — Certified negative-witness search on a dense test family

### 1. Operator used

**CHANGE-THE-OBJECT.** Replace attempts to manufacture a positive operator from a finite truncation by an auditable certificate problem for the already-defined complete quadratic form. The exact convention is the normalization block in `quadratic_positivity_search.md`: for real even compactly supported smooth h, g=h*tilde(h),

Q(h)=M_g(1/2)+M_g(-1/2) - sum_{n>=2} Lambda(n)n^(-1/2)(g(log n)+g(-log n)) -(log(4 pi)+gamma_E)g(0) - integral_0^infinity (g(x)+g(-x)-2 exp(-x/2)g(0))*exp(x/2)/(exp(x)-exp(-x)) dx.

The prime sum is finite for each h. No zero locations are used to define or evaluate this arithmetic-side expression.

### 2. Generate three candidates

**14A — Dense rational-bump negative certificate.** Fix a nonnegative compactly supported C-infinity mollifier eta with integral one. Enumerate finite rational linear combinations of rational translates and rational positive dilates of eta, symmetrized to be real and even. For each candidate h, compute a rigorous interval enclosure for Q(h), using the finite prime sum exactly and outward-rounded enclosures for the pole, constants, and regularized gamma integral. Output a certificate only when the upper endpoint is strictly negative.

**14B — Robin finite-verification certificate.** Search integers n for a certified violation of sigma(n)<exp(gamma_E)n log log n, using rational interval bounds for both sides. This is a finite witness if a violating n is found; checking any finite initial range supplies no certificate for the universal inequality.

**14C — Increasing dense Gram hierarchy.** For the first m enumerated bump functions h_1,...,h_m, form the exact/interval-certified Hermitian matrix G_m with entries Q(h_i,h_j), and check every principal block for a negative eigenvalue. A negative vector in one block is a finite countercertificate; nonnegative blocks for every tested m are only finite checks.

### 3. Fast kill

- **14A:** Develop the exact one-sided certificate property below. It can terminate on a strict negative direction, but has no terminating success certificate when Q is nonnegative.
- **14B:** Killed as an independent route: Robin's all-integers assertion is itself an RH-equivalent global criterion; finite checking merely tests finitely many integers.
- **14C:** Killed as a positivity certificate: positivity on each finite span implies positivity on the completion only after checking every m, which is the original universal assertion. A finite m can miss a negative direction outside its span. Strict negativity, if present, is already handled by 14A.

### 4. Develop 14A

Let D_R be the real even C-infinity functions supported in [-R,R], with its usual test-function topology. The family of finite rational combinations of rational translates/dilates of eta is countable and dense in D_R after allowing rational support radii and arbitrarily fine dilations: convolution with eta_epsilon approximates a test function in every fixed finite collection of derivative seminorms, and the convolution integral is approximated by finite Riemann sums; rational perturbations of centers and coefficients preserve that approximation. Since Q is a continuous distributional quadratic form on each fixed support class (as follows termwise from the finite prime sum and the subtracted, locally integrable gamma kernel), strict negativity is open there.

Therefore:

If there exists h in D_R with Q(h)<0, choose epsilon=-Q(h)/4>0. Density gives an enumerated rational bump v with |Q(v)-Q(h)|<epsilon, hence Q(v)<-3epsilon<0. For that fixed v, the prime sum is finite; effective interval integration of the smooth pole and regularized gamma terms, with an analytic tail bound, eventually produces an enclosure [L_v,U_v] with U_v<0. The enumeration plus precision-refinement procedure eventually halts and emits the finite certificate (v,[L_v,U_v]).

Conversely, any emitted certificate U_v<0 is a valid disproof of Q>=0 on all tests. Thus 14A is a sound and complete *semidecision for strict failure*: it halts iff a negative test exists, subject to the stated effective interval-enclosure implementation. It is not a decision procedure and does not certify the nonnegative case. This is a general computable-analysis consequence of density and openness of strict negativity, not a new arithmetic positivity theorem.

Numerical audit of the already archived first-prime bump from Pass 12 was rerun at 50 decimal digits using two independent gamma-integral partitions. For h(x)=exp(-1/(1-(x/0.49)^2)^2) on |x|<0.49 and zero otherwise, both evaluations returned Q(h)=0.000125395798571955035372185087167614677036997075558, prime contribution -0.0000178871777611989264071317323179451351840608266676, and g(0)=0.0486552089173526486950285461405693947990014872321. This is positive for this particular h and so is not a negative certificate. The run reproduces the archived value; it does not provide a new global estimate or a machine-checkable interval proof.

### 5. Source audit

The exact Q convention and the earlier high-precision bump value are taken from the normalization block in `quadratic_positivity_search.md`; Pass 12 records the support localization log(2)<0.98<log(3), so only n=2 contributes to that bump's prime sum. The new mathematical deduction is the one-sided semidecision implication from (i) density in the fixed-support test topology, (ii) continuity of Q, and (iii) effective enclosures for the explicit terms. No numerical zero data, Xi values, or zero ordinates are used. The actual interval-enclosure program has not been implemented or formally verified here, so the computability claim is a mathematical algorithm specification conditional on standard effective evaluation of the displayed constants and integrals, not a delivered certificate generator.

### 6. Discrimination test

| Toy world | Result | Exact step |
|---|---|---|
| (a) Curves over finite fields | **No canonical transfer** | A rational bump in the real logarithmic test space has no specified map to curve cohomology or Frobenius eigenvalues; the certificate is only a sign test for the given Q. |
| (b) Ihara zeta of Ramanujan graphs | **No Ramanujan transfer** | A negative principal block of a separately defined Weil-form matrix is not an adjacency eigenvalue bound; nonnegative finite blocks do not certify the graph's Ramanujan property. |
| (c) Random Euler products | **Not phase-selective** | The test evaluates fixed von-Mangoldt atom weights and the gamma/pole terms. It supplies no constraint on independent Euler phases; changing phases does not alter this prescribed certificate problem. |
| (d) Davenport–Heilbronn | **Unavailable canonically** | The certificate uses the integer Euler-product coefficients Lambda(n). The no-Euler-product toy supplies no canonical coefficients for this Q, so importing the integer Q would change the object under test. |
| (e) Beurling false world | **At most one-sided detection** | If that model's own explicit-form criterion supplies a negative test, the same dense-enumeration logic can detect it. If its relevant form is nonnegative on the tested family, the search does not terminate; the procedure gives no terminating certificate that distinguishes a positive integer model from every false generalized-prime model. |

### 7. Verdict

**DEAD as a positivity mechanism; PARTIAL as a counterexample-search method.** The dense family plus certified upper bounds would eventually expose any strict negative test, but cannot halt with a proof of universal nonnegativity. Candidate 14C has the same one-sided limitation, and 14B transfers the target to an RH-equivalent universal inequality. No P1-P5 object is constructed; no determinant or Xi identification follows. The useful exact result is only the semidecision theorem stated above.

### 8. New-idea check

Enumerating a dense test family and using interval arithmetic to certify a strict violation are standard methods. The pass makes the logical asymmetry explicit for this complete arithmetic form and separates a sound negative certificate from an unavailable positive certificate. This is not a new positivity theorem, and the interval arithmetic implementation remains undone.

### 9. Lesson

A finite, auditable certificate can refute universal positivity when it finds one strict negative direction. Density and continuity do not turn finite nonnegative checks into a positive certificate; a two-sided terminating method needs additional structure that compresses the infinitely many tests into a proved finite bound.

### 10. Seed

Find an independently defined finite-dimensional invariant or coercive remainder estimate that bounds every omitted test direction uniformly, supplies a terminating certificate on the nonnegative side, and still fails in the stipulated false toy worlds; do not encode the universal Weil inequality as the certificate condition. Next operator: **IMPORT**.

## Pass 15 — Brownian Green pairing on logarithmic prime-power sites

### 1. Operator used

**IMPORT.** Import the Dirichlet-energy/Cameron–Martin Green kernel (G(s,t)=\min(s,t)) on the positive log-place ray. The arithmetic sites are (t_n=\log n=k\log p) for (n=p^k), with exact Weil prime coefficient (c_n=\Lambda(n)/\sqrt n=\log p/p^{k/2}). This gives a concrete positive Gram kernel whose diagonal is exactly (c_n), and a canonical anchor whose cross term can be checked against the complete prime contribution.

### 2. Generate three candidates

**15A — Brownian log-place Gram and anchor.** In (L^2(0,U)), set (v_{p^k}(u)=(k p^{k/2})^{-1/2}{\bf1}_{[0,k\log p]}(u)). Define (K_{mn}=\langle v_m,v_n\rangle), and use translations (T_t h(x)=h(x+t)). Claim: a common anchor (w_U(u)), fixed by the Dirichlet-energy geometry rather than by (Q), makes the cross term of a positive tensor square equal the complete finite Weil prime contribution, while the positive completion is controlled by the pole and gamma terms.

**15B — Finite-place degree-zero covariance.** For a finite prime set (I), let (D_I=\operatorname{diag}(\log p)_{p\in I}), (b_I=(\log p)_{p\in I}), and (L_I=\sum_{p\in I}\log p). Define (H_I=D_I-b_Ib_I^T/L_I), the weighted covariance after projecting away the degree direction. Claim: this imported degree-zero/Hodge projection has a canonical local extension to prime powers whose positive form is the Weil arithmetic block.

**15C — Vertical-fiber intersection matrix.** On the explicit arithmetic surface (X=\mathbb P^1_{\mathbb Z}), take the vertical fibers (F_p=X_{\mathbb F_p}) and their finite-place intersection matrix (J_{pq}=F_p\cdot F_q). Claim: after pairing with the standard polarization \(\overline{\mathcal O(1)}\), this matrix supplies the prime-power coefficients (\Lambda(p^k)/p^{k/2}) and the missing archimedean completion.

### 3. Fast kill

- **15A:** Develop the strongest candidate: both the Gram kernel and the anchor are explicit, and their cross term can be compared term by term with the exact normalized prime sum.
- **15B:** Killed at the data test: (H_I\) is a generic weighted covariance, positive by Cauchy–Schwarz for any choice of positive site weights, and contains neither the prime-power (k\)-dependence nor the gamma/pole completion.
- **15C:** Killed at the finite-place intersection calculation: distinct fibers are disjoint and a fiber has self-intersection zero in this product fibration, so (J_{pq}=0) for all p,q; this block cannot yield the nonzero von-Mangoldt coefficients. Adding metric terms is extra data and does not repair the zero finite-fiber block canonically.

### 4. Develop 15A

For (n=p^k\), put (t_n=k\log p), (c_n=\log p/p^{k/2}), and
\[
v_n(u)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{k p^{k/2}}}{\bf1}_{[0,t_n]}(u).
\]
Then the exact inner products are
\[
K_{mn}=\langle v_m,v_n\rangle
=\frac{\min(t_m,t_n)}{\sqrt{k_m k_n}\,p_m^{k_m/4}p_n^{k_n/4}},\qquad K_{nn}=t_n\frac1{k p^{k/2}}=c_n.
\]
Thus every finite K is positive semidefinite by its explicit (L^2) Gram representation, and its diagonal exactly matches the magnitude of the corresponding Weil prime coefficient.

For the prime-only finite sets requested in this pass, let (S=\{\infty\}\cup I) and (I\subset\{2,3,5,\ldots\}). Interpret (\infty) as the Dirichlet boundary (u=0), not as an extra matrix row; for (p,q\in I),
\[
K^{\rm Br}_{S,I}(p,q)=\langle p^{-1/4}{\bf1}_{[0,\log p]},q^{-1/4}{\bf1}_{[0,\log q]}\rangle
=(pq)^{-1/4}\min(\log p,\log q).
\]
At 60-digit working precision, the matrices and largest eigenvalues are:

```text
S={infinity,2}; I={2}
K = [0.49012907173427359585695086181761669064573034954953]
lambda_max = 0.49012907173427359585695086181761669064573034954953

S={infinity,2,3}; I={2,3}
K = [[0.49012907173427359585695086181761669064573035,
      0.44288161124652298635793929925376287535112107],
     [0.44288161124652298635793929925376287535112107,
      0.63428410059756397718638777254464905519953670]]
eigenvalues = [0.11349811770676396589537773730511468275814649,
               1.01091505462507360714796089705715106308712056]
lambda_max = 1.01091505462507360714796089705715106308712056

S={infinity,2,3,5}; I={2,3,5}
K = [[0.49012907173427359585695086181761669064573035,
      0.44288161124652298635793929925376287535112107,
      0.38978530406803381562315996319152419192898642],
     [0.44288161124652298635793929925376287535112107,
      0.63428410059756397718638777254464905519953670,
      0.55824088139735748674071041165030529710602916],
     [0.38978530406803381562315996319152419192898642,
      0.55824088139735748674071041165030529710602916,
      0.71976251555360049121866307327840345267889578]]
eigenvalues = [0.08371421077895237298863730843600928409804224,
               0.20074574836812490108858975854671514087172100,
               1.55971572873836079018477464065794477355439959]
lambda_max = 1.55971572873836079018477464065794477355439959
```

So this candidate's raw Green Gram is below 1 for the one-prime set, but exceeds 1 as soon as p=3 is added; it does not have a unit-contraction margin on the listed family.

There is an exact finite-cutoff cross-term factorization as well. Fix (X\ge2), let (P_X=\{p^k\le X\}), (U=\log X), (t_2=\log2), and define the finite-energy anchor
\[
w_U(u)=\begin{cases}1/\sqrt{t_2},&0\le u\le t_2,\\1/(2\sqrt u),&t_2<u\le U.\end{cases}
\]
Its cumulative integral is ∫_0^t w_U(u)\,du=\sqrt t for every (t\in[t_2,U]), and
\[
\|w_U\|_2^2=1+\frac14\log(U/t_2).
\]
For real even (h\), set (g=h*\widetilde h),
\[
F_{X,h}=\sum_{n\in P_X}\sqrt{c_n}\,T_{t_n}h\otimes v_n,
\qquad A_{U,h}=h\otimes w_U.
\]
Since \(\langle v_n,w_U\rangle=\sqrt{c_n}\) and \(\langle T_{t_n}h,h\rangle=g(t_n)\),
\[
\langle F_{X,h},A_{U,h}\rangle=\sum_{n\in P_X}c_n g(t_n).
\]
The normalized Weil prime term is exactly
\[
Q_{\rm prime,X}(h)=-2\sum_{n\in P_X}c_n g(t_n)
=\|F_{X,h}-A_{U,h}\|^2-\|F_{X,h}\|^2-\left(1+\frac14\log\frac{U}{\log2}\right)\|h\|_2^2.
\]
This identity is exact at every finite cutoff. The candidate's positive square therefore comes with two subtracted energies. The anchor cost grows as \(\frac14\log\log X+O(1)\), and the channel self-energy expands into ratio-lag correlations (g(\log(n/m))). No proved inequality absorbs both costs into the pole-plus-archimedean terms, uniformly in support. The factorization represents the prime term; it does not establish the sign of the complete Q.

### 5. Source audit

The positive kernel \(\min(s,t)\) is the standard Brownian/Cameron–Martin reproducing kernel, with feature map (t\mapsto{\bf1}_{[0,t]}\); see [Cameron–Martin/RKHS notes](https://mathweb.ucsd.edu/~bdriver/Cornell%20Summer%20Notes%202010/Lecture_Notes/Heat%20kernel%20weighted%20L2%20spaces_ver2.pdf). The arithmetic Hodge-index theorem for arithmetic surfaces is a different pairing: Faltings–Hriljac identify its degree-zero intersection form with the negative Néron–Tate height pairing under specific geometric hypotheses; see [arXiv:1810.06342](https://arxiv.org/abs/1810.06342). This candidate imports the Brownian Dirichlet Green kernel, not an established Arakelov intersection pairing. Its diagonal match is imposed by the explicit feature scaling, and its cross-term identity is direct algebra. No zero locations or target positivity are used. The obstruction is the exact subtraction in the square completion; there is no source-audited estimate for that remainder.

### 6. Discrimination test

| Toy world | Result | Exact step |
|---|---|---|
| (a) Curves over finite fields | **No Weil-bound transfer** | Replacing prime logs by closed-point degree logs gives another positive min-kernel Gram. Its positivity is not the curve's Frobenius degree pairing and does not bound Frobenius eigenvalues. |
| (b) Ihara zeta of Ramanujan graphs | **No Ramanujan transfer** | Replacing log-prime sites by cycle-length sites preserves the Gram identity for any graph; a non-Ramanujan graph still has this positive matrix. |
| (c) Random Euler products with independent phases | **Fails phase discrimination** | K uses only positive weights and log locations; independent local phases do not enter it, so its PSD property is unchanged. |
| (d) Davenport–Heilbronn | **No canonical construction** | The prescribed function has no Euler prime-power coefficients (c_n), so the arithmetic site list and diagonal matching are unavailable from its data. |
| (e) Beurling systems with bad zero behavior | **Fails discrimination** | Substitute generalized primes (q) and (c_{q^k}=\log q/q^{k/2}\); the same Brownian Gram, diagonal identity, and square completion hold regardless of off-line zeros. |

### 7. Verdict

**DEAD as a positivity mechanism.** The construction does give an exact finite Brownian-feature factorization of the linear Weil prime contribution as a cross term, but extracting it from the positive square subtracts \(\|F_{X,h}\|^2\) and a scale-dependent anchor energy; no complete-form inequality controls those terms. The raw finite Green matrix also crosses unit norm at (I=\{2,3\}). Its PSD proof persists in false generalized-prime models, so it fails the required discrimination test.

### 8. New-idea check

The kernel \(\min(s,t)\) and its indicator-function Gram representation are standard Brownian RKHS facts. The basic ``positive square with the linear prime term as a cross term'' also repeats the collective shift-defect construction in Pass 11 of `quadratic_positivity_search.md`, whose remainder ledger already records the self-interaction and scalar costs. This pass contributes a different explicit geometric feature map, its finite-cutoff exact anchor formula, and the three computed matrices; it is a candidate-specific repackaging, not a new positivity theorem or a claim of literature novelty.

### 9. Lesson

An imported Green pairing can tune its diagonal to von-Mangoldt magnitudes, but exact prime recovery as a cross term is insufficient: test the full square-completion cost against the complete pole/gamma form and the false generalized-prime model before treating the imported positivity as selective.

### 10. Seed

Can the Brownian anchor's explicit \(\frac14\log\log X\) growth be replaced by a finite-energy boundary vector through a different canonical Green kernel while preserving every prime-power overlap \(\langle v_{p^k},w\rangle=\sqrt{c_{p^k}}\), and can its residual energy be bounded by the exact archimedean term in false-world-discriminating fashion? Next operator: **COUNTEREXAMPLE-ANATOMY**.
## Pass 16 — Full-form boundary-layer anatomy at the first new prime atom

### 1. Operator used

**COUNTEREXAMPLE-ANATOMY.** Rather than infer a negative direction from the norm of a newly entering prime translation, isolate the exact support-boundary profile that could maximize that translation and evaluate its cost in the *complete* pole + prime + archimedean form. The candidate is local: it tests whether concentration at the first new atom can create a negative witness as support crosses its threshold.

### 2. Generate three candidates

**16A — First-atom endpoint blow-up.** Put (a_5=\tfrac12\log 5). For a real, even, nonnegative (phi\in C_c^\infty((-1,1))) with (|\phi|_2=1), define
\[
h_\varepsilon(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\varepsilon}}\left[\phi\!\left(\frac{x+a_5}{\varepsilon}\right)+\phi\!\left(\frac{x-a_5}{\varepsilon}\right)\right].
\]
It has unit (L^2)-norm and support radius (a_5+\varepsilon). Its autocorrelation is arranged to maximize the newly entered p=5 cross-translation; compare that exact gain against the gamma translation energy.

**16B — Minimal-support spectral crossing.** Define (\lambda(R)=\inf\{Q(h):h\in C_c^\infty([-R,R]),\ h\text{ real and even},\|h\|_2=1\}\). If the first radius with (\lambda(R)<0) existed, try to show the threshold is a zero eigenvalue of a support-restricted operator and extract its Euler–Lagrange equation.

**16C — Endpoint-adapted certified bump enumeration.** Extend the Pass 14A rational-bump semidecision by enumerating endpoint packets near each prime-power entry radius and applying rigorous interval bounds to (Q). A negative packet would be a finite certificate; a proof of positivity would require a finite stopping theorem.

### 3. Fast kill

- **16A:** Keep as the strongest candidate. It is an explicit smooth test family with an exact atom profile and an exact lower bound from the archimedean part of the complete form.
- **16B:** Killed as a threshold-crossing argument. Nesting makes \(\lambda(R)\) nonincreasing, but does not establish attainment or continuity at an atom-entry radius. In particular, a newly active truncated translation has nonvanishing operator norm immediately after entry, so a small-overlap perturbation argument cannot force \(\lambda(R_*)=0\). This repeats the `Atom-entry perturbation jump` and `Later-atom parity gaps unresolved by finite-section drift` constraints in `simplicity_search.md`.
- **16C:** Killed as a positivity route. It is the endpoint-focused version of Pass 14A: strict negativity is semidecidable, while a nonnegative run has no terminating certificate. It does not repair the `Dense rational-test enumeration gives only a one-sided negative-witness semidecision` failure class.

### 4. Develop 16A

Use the fixed exact normalization from `quadratic_positivity_search.md` and the Pass 15 decomposition. Write (g_\varepsilon=h_\varepsilon*\widetilde h_\varepsilon), (c_\Gamma=\gamma_E+\log\pi+3\log2+\pi/2), and
\[
Q(h_\varepsilon)=P(h_\varepsilon)+E_\Gamma(h_\varepsilon)-c_\Gamma\|h_\varepsilon\|_2^2-2\sum_{n\ge2}\frac{\Lambda(n)}{\sqrt n}g_\varepsilon(\log n),
\]
where (P(h_\varepsilon)=|\int e^{x/2}h_\varepsilon(x)\,dx|^2+|\int e^{-x/2}h_\varepsilon(x)\,dx|^2\ge0), and
\[
E_\Gamma(h)=\int_0^\infty \frac{e^{-u/2}}{1-e^{-2u}}\,\|h(\cdot+u)-h\|_2^2\,du\ge0.
\]

The two packet supports are centered at \(\pm a_5\), each with width \(2\varepsilon\), and are disjoint for \(\varepsilon< a_5\). Direct substitution gives \(\|h_\varepsilon\|_2^2=1\), (g_\varepsilon(0)=1), and
\[
g_\varepsilon(\log5)=\int h_\varepsilon(x+2a_5)h_\varepsilon(x)\,dx=\frac12.
\]
Moreover, the support of (g_\varepsilon) is contained in the union of the three autocorrelation intervals centered at (0,\pm\log5), each of radius (2\varepsilon). For (0<\varepsilon<10^{-2}), the only positive prime-power logarithm in this support is \(\log5\): the neighboring values are \(\log4\) and \(\log7\), separated from \(\log5\) by more than (0.22). Thus the entire prime term is exactly
\[
-2\frac{\log5}{\sqrt5}g_\varepsilon(\log5)=-\frac{\log5}{\sqrt5}.
\]

For (4\varepsilon\le u\le b=0.1), the translated packet supports are disjoint: same-packet overlap needs (u<2\varepsilon), and cross-packet overlap requires (u\) within (2\varepsilon) of \(\log5\). Hence (g_\varepsilon(u)=0) and \(\|h_\varepsilon(\cdot+u)-h_\varepsilon\|_2^2=2) throughout that interval. Since (1-e^{-2u}\le2u) and (e^{-u/2}\ge e^{-b/2}),
\[
E_\Gamma(h_\varepsilon)\ge2\int_{4\varepsilon}^{b}\frac{e^{-u/2}}{1-e^{-2u}}\,du\ge e^{-0.05}\log\!\left(\frac{0.1}{4\varepsilon}\right).
\]
Dropping the nonnegative pole term yields the rigorous inequality
\[
\boxed{Q(h_\varepsilon)\ge e^{-0.05}\log\!\left(\frac{0.1}{4\varepsilon}\right)-c_\Gamma-\frac{\log5}{\sqrt5}.}
\]
Therefore this endpoint-concentrating family has (Q(h_\varepsilon)\to+\infty) as \(\varepsilon\downarrow0\), even though its support radius tends to the first p=5 entry radius \(a_5\) from above and its p=5 prime correlation remains exactly (1/2). At 70-digit precision,

```text
c_Gamma = 5.3721834192256655822329574974497422890145392516235538431689
log(5)/sqrt(5) = 0.71976251555360049121866307327840345267889578238599213870496
support threshold log(5)/2 = 0.8047189562170501873003796666130938197628
for epsilon = 1e-20:
  Q(h_epsilon) >= 34.2048172424844087387953913283040857842822311341494021990636
```

This is a lower bound, not a numerical evaluation of the full (Q(h_\varepsilon)); it is independent of the particular admissible (phi). It rules out the most singular way to exploit the p=5 atom. It does not bound broader profiles that occupy the interior of the support or combine several prime-power translations.

### 5. Source audit

The complete-form normalization and the identity (Q_\Gamma=E_\Gamma-c_\Gamma\|h\|_2^2) are taken from `quadratic_positivity_search.md` and Pass 15 of this ledger. The packet autocorrelation, exact p=5 coefficient, disjoint-support statement, and logarithmic lower bound are derived directly above. The two failed candidates repeat documented limits: support-threshold perturbation requires an operator-norm estimate (`Atom-entry perturbation jump`, `simplicity_search.md`), and dense bump enumeration cannot certify the nonnegative case (`Pass 14A`, this ledger). No zero locations or numerical zero data enter.

### 6. Discrimination test

| Toy world | Result | Exact step |
|---|---|---|
| (a) Curves over finite fields | **No transfer** | This proof uses the real logarithmic archimedean kernel (e^{-u/2}/(1-e^{-2u})); a function-field explicit formula has a different place-at-infinity term, with no supplied map carrying this endpoint estimate to Frobenius cohomology. |
| (b) Ihara zeta of Ramanujan graphs | **No graph criterion** | The estimate is a translation-energy bound on \\(\mathbb R\) with a continuous (u=0) singularity; graph cycle lengths do not supply this kernel or an adjacency spectral bound. |
| (c) Random Euler products | **Not phase-selective** | The lower bound uses only the nonnegative gamma energy, the scalar (c_\Gamma), and a bounded isolated atom. Replacing the atom coefficient by any phase of the same modulus leaves the logarithmic divergence and hence the boundary-family result unchanged. |
| (d) Davenport–Heilbronn | **No canonical atom** | Without the prescribed integer Euler product there is no canonical von Mangoldt atom at \(\log5\); importing this atom would change the model. |
| (e) Beurling systems with bad zero behavior | **Fails discrimination** | For any model with a finite isolated generalized-prime atom of bounded coefficient at location (t_0>0), the same two-packet construction has an (O(1)) atom term and the same (+\log(1/\varepsilon)) archimedean boundary cost. The local conclusion therefore survives without constraining the model's zeros. |

### 7. Verdict

**DEAD as a global positivity mechanism; PARTIAL as a counterexample-anatomy result.** The exact theorem is that this concentrated endpoint family cannot produce negative (Q) near the first p=5 atom: its full form tends to (+\infty), with the explicit lower bound above. Against the five target properties, it does not construct an exact multiplicative-to-additive bridge (P1); it proves positivity only on this special boundary family, not for all tests (P2); it uses an even test and the fixed explicit-formula normalization but supplies no new functional-equation operator (P3); it gives no determinant identity with \\(\Xi\) (P4); and it supplies no zero-as-spectral-data realization (P5). The result constrains one proposed source of negative tests but leaves nonconcentrating and multi-atom tests open.

### 8. New-idea check

The support-entry jump itself was already identified in the simplicity ledger. The new point is to test that jump inside the *complete* Weil form: the archimedean translation energy charges endpoint concentration by a logarithm that dominates the bounded new-atom gain. This is a candidate-specific exact inequality, not a general theorem that all tests are positive, and not a literature novelty claim.

### 9. Lesson

A fixed operator-norm contribution at an atom threshold does not imply a negative boundary-layer direction. Rescale the test itself: when endpoint localization forces the full archimedean energy to grow like (\log(1/\varepsilon)), the isolated prime cross term remains bounded. Any alleged negative anatomy at an atom-entry threshold must therefore use a nonconcentrating profile or coordinate several atoms against the gamma energy.

### 10. Seed

For support radius just beyond (\tfrac12\log p), classify all profiles by their mass in endpoint layers versus the interior. Can a quantitative concentration-compactness decomposition bound the p-atom correlation by the gamma translation energy for every profile, while permitting any residual negative direction to be converted into a finite, false-world-discriminating witness? Next operator: **IMPOSSIBILITY** (the next operator in the prescribed rotation).
## Pass 17 — Robin slack as a prime-exponent cocycle

### 1. Operator used

**IMPOSSIBILITY.** Assume a Robin-type failure and ask whether its normalized slack, tracked along the lattice of prime-exponent vectors, must make an impossible discrete jump. The test is whether exact local increments have a forced sign or nonzero circulation that conflicts with integer factorization.

### 2. Generate three candidates

**17A — Prime-append Robin-slack cocycle.** For (n>e), set (R(n)=\sigma(n)/(e^\gamma\log\log n)). When (q\nmid n) is prime, the edge (n\to nq) has an explicit logarithmic increment. Claim: at a least crossing from (R<1) to (R\ge1), the increment must violate an exact sign/size constraint imposed by the prime-exponent lattice.

**17B — Prime-square circulation obstruction.** On the exponent lattice, compare the two paths (n\to np\to npq) and (n\to nq\to npq) for distinct primes (p,q\nmid n). Claim: a hypothetical least violating vertex forces the signed edge increments around this square to have nonzero circulation, contradicting path independence.

**17C — Pole-cancelled positive divisor increments.** Apply a finite difference in (log n) to a nonnegative divisor-sum statistic to remove the smooth (e^\gamma\log\log n) barrier, then ask whether the remaining prime-power jumps keep one sign. Claim: this yields an integer-valued sign obstruction under a hypothetical violation.

### 3. Fast kill

- **17A:** Keep for the exact edge identity and a numerical sign audit on actual superabundant records.
- **17B:** Killed algebraically: the slack is a scalar potential, so every closed path telescopes to zero identically. A least violation does not change that identity.
- **17C:** Killed by sign loss: every nonzero finite-difference stencil annihilating a nonconstant smooth barrier has mixed-sign coefficients, so nonnegativity of the original divisor or prime sum supplies no sign for the difference. This repeats Pass 7B's exact obstruction.

### 4. Develop 17A

Let
\[
R(n)=\frac{\sigma(n)/n}{e^\gamma\log\log n},\qquad \rho(n)=\log R(n),\qquad L=\log n.
\]
For a prime (q\nmid n), multiplicativity gives
\[
\frac{\sigma(nq)}{nq}=\frac{\sigma(n)}n\left(1+\frac1q\right),
\]
while (loglog(nq)=\log(L+\log q)). Therefore the exact edge increment is
\[
\boxed{\delta_q(n):=\rho(nq)-\rho(n)
=\log\left(1+\frac1q\right)
-\log\left(\frac{\log(L+\log q)}{\log L}\right).}
\]
For (n>e), all logarithms in this expression are defined and positive where needed. Its sign is exactly characterized by
\[
\delta_q(n)>0
\iff \frac{\log L}{q}>\log\left(1+\frac{\log q}{L}\right)
\iff L^{1+1/q}>L+\log q.
\]
This is a local condition, not a global estimate. As a first-order heuristic only, for (L\) large relative to (log q),
\[
\delta_q(n)=\frac1q-\frac{\log q}{L\log L}
+O\!\left(q^{-2}+\frac{(\log q)^2}{L^2\log L}
+\frac{(\log q)^2}{L^2(\log L)^2}\right).
\]
The exact sign test above, not this asymptotic expansion, is used for the computations.

For distinct primes (p,q\nmid n), the square identity is
\[
\delta_p(n)+\delta_q(np)
=\rho(npq)-\rho(n)
=\delta_q(n)+\delta_p(nq).
\]
Thus its circulation is zero exactly. It cannot force a parity defect or contradictory sign by itself.

A sieve of (n\le300000) formed the strict record values of the exact rational statistic (\sigma(n)/n); it found nine records at or above 5040. For each record (n), (q) was the first prime larger than its largest prime factor, and the increment above was evaluated with 75-digit arithmetic. The complete output was:

```text
n       q       delta_q(n)                       direction
5040    11     -0.02243142364915967539824972282066663644454082916821529337  DOWN
10080   11     -0.01201237623164915745994428293972589642255112256463117417  DOWN
15120   11     -0.00671188436310847562669404353449907291181647378541770339  DOWN
25200   11     -0.00072335268834215352616380835323474214684246737019031461  DOWN
27720   13     -0.01775436587876037079599791197076132436486932762171175840  DOWN
55440   13     -0.01043610579894056545003218562131971686354446407548471619  DOWN
110880  13     -0.00410606529224308037526090723955885301662846737019031461  DOWN
166320  13     -0.00078697436417180808541875822396390040074048188567475941  DOWN
277200  13      0.00304778455486482041156733468172475171716611100682254130  UP
```

For the decisive sign change, the 80-digit ratio calculation gives

```text
n=277200, q=13:
R(n)   = 0.96079403540367220652887467709960618...
R(nq)  = 0.96372679556554586694914450163458885...
log(R(nq)/R(n))
       = 0.003047784554864820411567334681724751717166111006822541304...
```

For comparison, at (n=25200,q=11), the increment is (-0.000723352688342153526163808353234742146842467370190314614). The opposite signs are not numerical ambiguity: they follow from the exact threshold criterion and the displayed high-precision values. The positive increment at (277200\to3{,}603{,}600) does not cross 1; both displayed ratios remain below 1.
